ACDCs for Enterprises
Alan Davies

… and thank you all for being here. So, I've been accused many times in the past of oversimplifying. I can
say here and now that I never oversimplify. However I do like to simplify things and the world out there
needs to understand what KERI and ACDCs can deliver to the world and we need to find ways of simpli‐
fying our messages to them to make sure that they get it.
I thank Thomas [Mayfield, red.] for his great presentation explaining in high-level terms what KERI
is all about and then diving into the lower level detail and I'm going to a certain extent follow that track.
Let's get going.
I want to start by talking about how trust used to work. It used to be all about personal reputation.
This in the days when you wanted to carry out some kind of transaction, buy something, you would go
along to the person who was selling it or providing it. You would talk to them, you would make a deal,
you shake hands and you would decide whether you're trusting them or not. If you trusted them, you
would give them their money. They would give you the goods and oﬀ you went. And so trust depended
on physical presence.
All of the verifications that took place were pretty well immediate and you would decide do I really
trust this person? If so here's your money. I'll take the goods now or maybe later. Fraud did exist. Of
course there would be people who would promise to do something and then not deliver it but the scale
was limited to I guess the person that they were they were doing the deal with.
So that was in the distant past. Now things have changed and in fact they changed at least a couple of
hundred years ago when the ability to communicate in diﬀerent ways came along. But it they changed in
a very big way when global communications arrived firstly with the telephone and then Fax machines and
of course now the internet.
What the internet enabled us to be able to do is execute transactions remotely which is a great thing
in many ways. It enabled international commerce to be able to scale up in so many diﬀerent ways and
also allow regular humans to participate in that kind of commerce. We can go online to Amazon, we can
buy what we want when we want and it turns up the next day. It's very convenient.
In order to support that, when the internet came along, it was a almost a natural knee-jerk reaction to
say, well, if people are going to use online systems, they need to authenticate to be able to access it. And
usernames and passwords was the way that we did that.
So you log in, you get a session token and the system that you're talking to uses that token to decide
what you're allowed to do. The end result so far has been rampant online fraud and we've done what we
can to limit that. Mostly by trying to detect fraud which is in my view a losing battle. The fraudsters have
so many tools available to them to be able to fool the general public into thinking that they are someone
legitimate.
and detection is we're always going to be losing that battle to try and detect fraudulent activity. This
is my own personal view of the distant future in which everything that we do will be signed. So, it's a
signed intent world and I think the way we'll end up if you think maybe four or five years in the future
We will all have an agent on our devices that does things for us.
You'll ask your agent AI agent to do something. It'll be maybe move some money from your checking
account to your savings account. the AI agent on your device because it knows you've authenticated with
that device biometrically. It'll wrap that request in an ACDC, sign it with your private key.
7
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
It'll just use your face ID or whatever other biometric it's using to make sure it really is you. It may
add additional signatures if necessary. So if you're a child using your device, it may be that whatever
you're asking your agent to do requires your parents permission.
So, you may get an additional signature added to that request. That request may include relevant cre‐
dentials, your SEDI credential for example, or who knows what other entitlement credentials that you
may have. That request will then get sent to some kind of execution service. If it's your bank, because
you're moving money from one account to another, then it'll be your bank's online system.
It'll verify the trustability of that request. It'll see where it came from. It will see your signature. It will
see your maybe your parents signature if you're a child. It will do the usual KERI ACDC stuﬀ. Verify it
hasn't been tampered with.
They it they know it's you and the bank will then execute that request and make a record of it. And I
don't think there will be a need to have a username and password. You won't need to login for this be‐
cause basically you've sent a signed sealed request to your bank that they know it came from you. They'll
execute it.
They'll record it and that's the end of it. I'm exaggerating here. I'm sure there will be passwords, ses‐
sions tokens, and 2FA for all sorts of stuﬀ, but in the long term, they will be at least less necessary. And
in the example I gave you, you can imagine a world where everything you do is signed intent.
I think that this is going to be how we reduce the rampant online fraud to .., there's always going to
be fraud, unfortunately, there always going to be someone who will find a way of fooling someone else
into doing something they shouldn't be doing. But this is the long-term vision and this is all going to be
delivered, in my view, with implementations of KERI and ACDCs, and everything that we've been talking
about today.
However, there's going to be a lot of disruption to be able to enable all of that. I mean the example I
gave you about the bank they need to rearchitect their systems so that they are accepting signed requests
from their customers that they identify through their their online identity that the customers own and
that's going to be a great world of course everybody will be their own identity provider which is how it
should be if you think about it. I'm the only person who knows that I am me. And if I control a digital
identity that asserts that, then I'm by far the best person to be able to prove who I am.
And I don't like the idea of delegating that to some other federated identity provider. However, they're
not going to like it at all. The federated identity providers are having lots of information from people
every time they log in. So, there's going to be a lot of resistance.
Information harvesters are going to hate all of this because everything that Sam [Smith, red.] tells us
that we should be in control of is not what they want us to be in control of, sadly. As I mentioned, all
sorts of online services will need to do a lot of rearchitecting to implement that final vision.
And this isn't going to happen in the next 12 months. There will be movement towards it but In my
view, we need to do something sooner to start getting KERI and ACDC adoption in a small way and then
move on. So this is where we are now. We have rampant online fraud.
Autonomous AI agents are doing more and more stuﬀ and working out how to do things on their
own. Mostly because they think that they are doing what we want them to do but not necessarily. The
current way of recording what happens is really online logging. So the the bank will keep a record of what
8
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
you asked it to do moving money from one account to another. But if someone gets my password and
asks my bank to do something on behalf of me then as far as the bank logging is concerned it was me do‐
ing it which just is not real. So it is it's just weak.
So what we really need is what I call trustability. And trustability is the ability to be able to make a
good trust decision on any kind of action that you might be Intending to make based on some informa‐
tion that comes at you over the internet. And it can be something as simple as a news article.
Did this journalist really write this? Do I trust this journalist? The trust decision might be very weak.
I'm just going to develop my own opinion about whatever is being written about based on based on that
journalist's reputation. But other things are much more important.
If I see a request from one of my kids to send some money to a bank account because he's in trouble,
then I need to be able to trust that is really from my son. And I need to make a trust decision. I might
need to make it quickly. So how do we get trustability into the world of online transactions?
And what we need of course is KERI and ACDCs and we need .., I hate to say "a little bit of KERI and
ACDC" because we need the whole thing but we can't very quickly implement the entire or reimplement
the entire online access infrastructure to support the the long-term vision of how the whole world of
signed intent is going to work. And where digital trust foundry is starting, is saying that what we need is
something that we call execution trust.
Immediately before making some kind of Decision to execute some kind of action based on informa‐
tion that's coming to us remotely. And that might be we're a supplier receiving a purchase order. We
might be an employer being presented with someone's academic credentials before we hire them for a
job. Before we make that decision, then we need a way to be able to make a trust assessment of what's
being presented to us.
And this is where I think that what the world needs is to be able to start slowly and to be able to im‐
plement what we call execution trust into existing systems and of course start with high value high-risk
transactions. For example wire transfer fraud. It is way too easy for bad actors to be able to make changes
to wire transfer requests as they move from place to face before they get to the bank that's going to exe‐
cute that wire transfer.
Taking a wire transfer request, wrapping it in an ACDC, getting it signed by the owner of the origi‐
nating account means that when the bank gets it, they can verify it, make a trust decision and execute it
and then keep a cryptographically verifiable record of what they were asked to execute for any time in the
future. So what we're talking about is inserting a level of trustability into existing applications at execu‐
tion time, hence the term execution trust.
And this can apply to any digital artifact. Verifiable credentials was really all about academic and pro‐
fessional credentials but ACDCs are really capable of handling any kind of data content that might be de‐
livered from one trust domain to another.
Even better in the short term, it makes sense even with a single trust domain to be able to take some
kind of action. Let's say, it's updating a production system. We have an update that we want to make to a
production system and we want to make sure that it's fully authorized before we execute it. And of
course, that update may have been generated by AI. So it's kind of important that a human gets involved
in that process. A workflow application that's going to apply that update to the production system could
9
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
take that description of that update request, wrap it in an ACDC, send it to the CIO to say, "We're about
to update your production systems with this. Do you approve it?" He would sign it, return it back. Then
that update application would say " Okay, I have approval to go ahead with with this update, apply the
update, keep a record" and that all happens within a single trust domain. What you've done there is ap‐
plied a little bit of KERI and ACDCs to a particular high value use case to make sure that in that particu‐
lar example the update was being made was fully authorized by someone who is the appropriate autho‐
rizer of that update.
The same thing applies across trust domains where maybe a business generates a legal document that
needs to be signed by the CEO before it gets delivered to a partner for them to execute. You can build
that kind of functionality into an existing application that's already generating these documents. You just
say, "Well, we already have the document. It's a PDF.
We're just going to wrap it in ACDC, get it signed, and send it oﬀ. And then whoever gets it, because
they know how to verify an ACDC, we'll run a verification. They know that it was signed by the CEO,
they can act on it.
I think that's basically what I'm talking about here. And of course, multisig comes in very useful in
situations like this. The application at execution time can verify and either decide to reject it if it's not
happy with what it sees or executed and keep a permanent record of its trust decision and what it made
that trust decision based on.
The benefits of this approach which is a little bit of KERI and ACDCs injected into existing business
applications for high value transactions. There are so many business use cases that this can apply to
across all industries.
I know from personal experience that if I use my bank's online system to do a wire transfer even
though I'm logged in, it'll get to a certain point where it says " We're going to send you a code to your mo‐
bile phone and want you to type it in. So, two-factor authentication to make sure that even though I'm al‐
ready logged in that it really is me again, sort of really me.
This would replace those kinds of weak two-factor authentication with what we call a hard gate.
There will be no need to rearchitect existing systems. If you take for example the bank's wire transfer re‐
quest system, they already have that ability in their system. It's part is the functionality of their banking
system anyway. The additional step would be just to make an API call to a platform that knows how to
wrap that content into an ACDC, send it to an authorized signer, and return the signed ACDC back.
Then that application just makes another API call to verify it, looks at the results, makes a trust decision,
either rejects or continues. No need to completely rearchitect existing systems.
This will drastically reduce online fraud for high value business transactions and it can be done really
very soon. I like to talk about this as getting used to KERI and ACDCs one bite at a time. So you intro‐
duce something like this to a high value transaction that's already being executed by business ap‐
plications Today and they see the immediate benefits and then once businesses start using this technolo‐
gy in this limited constrained way then they will be able to see how that the basic technology can be ap‐
plied in all sorts of other situations. And this is how we get the world starting to use KERI and ACDCs
without having to make huge changes.

And also this is another good reason for businesses to get a vLEI of course. Digital Trust Foundry is a

(let me see if I'm getting ahead of myself here) We've implemented what we call a trustables API. So

The end result is, it gets back an ACDC that it can verify and that's where we're starting. We're saying

We're looking for partners. Our business model is "Yes, we will deliver our a platform" for use by

network of businesses who are implementing the power of ACDCs and KERI without having to build the
whole thing themselves. They just make use of our platform. So in some ways it's similar to if you're a
business wanting to Manage your sales process, you can either use spreadsheets and build your own ap‐
plication to manage all of your sales processes or you can go to Salesforce and sign up for a CRM with
them. they provide the functionality all of the low-level detail and you just get the benefits.
We have a list of interactive examples of our functionality that you can go and play with and just to
give you some ideas of the sorts of things that can be done with that. There's about 30 diﬀerent use cases
in diﬀerent businesses. One of my favorites is verifiable résumés in which an individual who's collected
digital credentials or professional credentials can actually take them all and generate a verifiable resume
that has references to all of each of those credentials. and they can provide their digital verifiable resume
to a potential employer who can actually click verify on that resume. It will go to the issuers of each of
those credentials, verify them and then return a trust report to the employer.
That's all I have to say for now. Thank you very much for your time and if you have any questions
now is the time. – I got one question because of the term that you coined "trustability." I use that in a

I heard your explanation of trustability but could we use the word trustworthiness as well in your…
Of course, yes. The ability to be able to make a trust decision on something is my definition of trustabili‐
ty which may not be a real word. But I think it should be.
We're making trust decisions all of the while. But when I receive some kind of digital information
over the internet that I want to make a trust decision on then I have, at the moment, only two choices.
One's called blind faith. Well, there's three actually.
Blind faith. The one is ignore it completely. And the third is spend quite a lot of time doing some

KRAM IT! with Ari
Ari Argoud
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · TALKS · 29:13

KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Hi everybody. This is KRAM It! with Ari. Important exclamation mark! placement there. So…
KRAM is KERI Request Authentication Mechanism and this is for all non-KEL events or non-key
event messages that occur in the KERI protocol and some examples include encrypt sender sign
receiver which if you were at Phil's [talk] you'd know is a good way of doing Secure APIs to a given iden‐
tifier essentially a replacement for shared-secret-based account management.
You can do exchange transactions for negotiating contracts or other negotiations. You can do creden‐
tial presentations. And so the fundamental problem that KRAM solves is ensuring that a message is
timely and authentic. So without KRAM, an attacker can intercept a valid signed message and then replay
that message at a later time and you could have a ..., or the receiver of that message would have no way
of distinguishing it from a fresh one which could cause all kinds of problems, like if you were signing on
to an account or something, you could have someone who is able to sign on for you. So KRAM works by
enforcing a timeliness cache window.
Each message includes a datetime stamp and the receiver is going to check whether that time stamp
falls within an acceptable [time] window relative to its own clock. If the message has been seen before, if
it falls outside the window, it's just going to get dropped.
So KRAM exists at the top of the message processing pipeline. All of the non-key event message flow
through a non-key event messages flow through a single method and KRAM is applied immediately after
some basic 'allow deny' logic if in case you want to filter for given identifiers, in case you want to not let
specific people in. And I should note here that KRAM is designed for KERI v2 which is not out yet. [up‐
date July26: v2.0 is out] But it represents a change from a prior approach wherein before we would es‐
crow messages when authentication information wasn't immediately available like if we didn't have the
Key Event Log whereas now we just drop them and notify such that the Key Event Log can be retrieved
via something that is out of scope for this talk.
So before diving into KRAM's mechanics I just want to make sure that everybody is familiar or largely
familiar with these few key terminology points. Does anyone have any questions about KERI? Well, okay.
Questions about KERI might be a little broad, but we all know what AIDs are, KELs, Key State, we've
talked a lot about it today. SAID is maybe worth touching on, which is the Self-Addressing Identifiers,
which are digests computed over the contents of a data structure.
And basically, this is an ID for a given message or in this instance, it is or referencing it as an ID for a
given message. – So it's a context identifier based on your payload? – Yeah, that's that seems correct. – It's
actually the whole message, right? – It's a digest of the message.
– It's a digest of the message? – But it's embedded in the message… […] protocol. So that there's one
universal identifier for that message. – So it's unique per message even if applied in a thread. – Yeah. –
Yes. – Could you repeat the conclusion of this because you are the only one with a microphone.
– Oh yes. So basically We have SAIDs as unique identifiers which are digests of a given message such
that no two messages that are the same would have diﬀerent sets. I.e. if two messages have the same set
then they must have identical contents and time stamps. So then Seals, briefly is a cryptographic commit‐
ment which can anchor arbitrary data to a tree of hashes or to a particular event in the key-event se‐
quence. And we're assuming with KRAM that the receiver will already hold the copy of the sender's KEL.
Which is again kind of gesturing towards that fact that that retrieval mechanism is out of scope but it
does exist or will exist.
14
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So in KRAM we have three authentication types. We have an Anchoring Seal Reference which is a
message that's authenticated via a seal in the sender's KEL. And this works because you can preemptively
authenticate your messages as a sender by digesting them into a seal with the authentications already
created and then adding that to your KEL, and then continuing your Key Event Log. I think this would be
done via an interaction event and then other people can just look up that seal and confirm that is a pre-
authenticated message. We have Single-Key Signature which is just one key in the key list. I should men‐
tion that, let's see… when we are determining this type of authentication we're looking at key list
cardinality.
So sometimes an identifier will have multiple signing keys but it won't be a multi key signature and
for the purposes in .., eventually that matters, it is covered in KRAM because KRAM does authenticate
but what we're doing here, why we're breaking these things out is because… … we're trying to do
Amplification and DoS (Denial of Service) mitigation. So, it's important to do the cheapest method of
dropping messages before we get into the more expensive authentication-based methods of dropping
messages.
So, first we would… first we're going to look at the key list cardinality. We're going to say is it a single
signature? Is it a multi key signature? Does it have a seal reference? And then if it's multi key and it has a
seal reference, we're going to try and look at that seal reference first before we go on to see if that multi‐
key has some has some validity to it, if it has signatures that are valid and validating those.
All right. So we'll talk briefly about multisig. You guys have probably heard it all already today. But
this is when multiple signing keys are required to authorize an action. These would be distributed sign‐
ing keys. In practice, that's when a multisig group sends a message and not all the signatures may be at‐
tached at once. The diﬀerent members of the group may sign at diﬀerent times and from diﬀerent de‐
vices. The receiver needs to collect those signatures, needs to collect those signatures, sorry incremental‐
ly until we meet a threshold which will validate that message if the message is able to collect all those
signatures.
So this is where we have diﬀerent KRAM [time] windows. KRAM uses a long lag window for multi
key signatures wherein as opposed to a short lag window for single keys and seal reference messages. The
long lag might be minutes, might be hours or days whatever is appropriate for the operational tempo of
the multisig group and the short lag only accounts for network latency and initial processing times. This
is that window that I was mentioning earlier.
It's going to be diﬀerent depending on the authentication mechanism that we suss out in the begin‐
ning of the KRAM-It method, which is why this [talk] is called 'KRAM It with Ari.' So, let's talk about
what a Replay Attack is I mentioned this already but I wanted to go through it again just so that we can
create some context for the next things that we're going to be talking about. So, Replay Attacks are fun‐
damental threats in any authenticated messaging system. The concept is that an attacker will intercept a
message that was legitimately created and signed and then is able to resend that replaying it to the re‐
ceiver at a later time. If the receiver has no mechanism to detect that the message is a duplicate, it may
process it again as if it were fresh. And depending on the message type, this could grant unauthorized ac‐
cess, trigger duplicate operations, or otherwise cause harm. So, it's important to note that the attacker
doesn't need to forge or modify anything in the case of a replay attack. The message is genuinely signed
by the real sender and the attacker can exploit the fact that message has a valid signature on it. But that
doesn't mean that the message is provably delivered right now.
15
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So this is why timeliness is a core mechanic for KRAM. A message could be properly signed but it
must also fall within the timeliness window that is able to correctly filter out any messages that may be
sent by malicious bad actors.
– In current-use protocols, how is that mitigated? - OAuth2 or other examples – I don't know. I've
only studied KERI, so I couldn't tell you how OAuth 2.0 does it. [inaudible] approach in most secure
channel – is it a nonce – by TLS is you do a challenge response.
So it's you […] with three messages. So you send a request, the host sends you back a nonce, you
sign the nonce or encrypt the nonce, send it back and then they decrypt it or verify the signature and that
nonce is basically not replayable as long as they don't ever generate the same nonce and accept it. That
means they either assume an ephemeral secure session so they don't have to save the nonces but if you
want something like multisig that can span multiple sessions then your host because now you have to
store all those nonces forever get replayed.
So, when you look at Replay Attack Protection, when you go away from ephemeral interactions to
things that have to last for long periods of time, most of the existing mechanisms just don't work. They
just don't scale. You have to remember things forever or you have to start to build provable caches.
– Statistically random with large – Yeah, – That makes sense actually. – Okay. – I want to ask to sum‐
marize. To summarize… other authentication mechanisms like OAuth 2.0 do multi- message authentica‐
tion via nonces which are not scalable as such.
Now, talk about some fundamental protections of KRAM. So, the first thing that we're going to do
when well, we're ordering these by the cheapest means of making sure that your message is authenticat‐
ed per the given point in the protection that you're at. So the first thing that we want to do is we want to
look up this message. Like we said, it's got a SAID on it which is unique. So we're looking it up against a
cache. And if that message is there in that cache already, then we know it's a duplicate because no mes‐
sage can have the same set as a message that's already existed. So you know you've got something funny
going on if you're… or well it could be it could not be something funny. It could also just be someone
who's legitimately trying to send a message again. But the point being that the first thing we do cache
lookup cache lookup and drop if we already have something there. We are also checking to see if these
are multikey messages because we can have a message that has the same SAID but has additional signa‐
tures that must be collected towards a threshold. So there is a little bit of nuance with respects to multi-
key messages.
Next, you're going to do a timeliness check which only occurs for uncashed messages. And you're go‐

wherein you're going to be waiting for an even longer time to do diﬀerent negotiations of contracts or
multiple messages within within a given very long time window.
So right, do you see here ML equals SL, i.e. we're doing for seal reference and single key we swap this

H i, my name is Ari Rgoode. I am a full -stack Python developer with HealthKERI, and my presenta‐

diﬀerently is I'm really engaged with moving the healthcare industry specifically towards a repudi‐

Previously, everybody had their own bespoke methods for doing this when implementing with
ShareAPI, and it is very beneficial for the ecosystem to leave everybody into the same modality of protec‐

I' m Cole Davis. I am the founder and CEO of SwitchCord. I'm a lawyer and technologist and I spoke
today on what do we call it? Contract credentials and verifiable legal authority. So modeling legal re‐
lationships stuck in paper-based agreements, PDFs, Word documents into KERI, ACDC spec to be
proving your contractual authority to third parties. So I see things diﬀerently now after living in this
world for the past six, seven years. That contractual authority and your ability to go through life and do
things depends on legal contracts and authority from the state and you can't get away from it. And so I
stopped viewing these things as separate and it's all sort of this interrelated mix of identity and legal.
And that led to me also seeing finance accounting, legal finance and accounting is not separate disci‐
plines like I was taught and have degrees in, but it's really a spectrum. And identity, I found, is the one
thing that ties all of them to together. It is the backbone of commercial transactions. And I think people
are starting to come around to that idea. I've been saying it for a long time and it's exciting to see people,
their eyes light up when they realize what I'm talking about. So if my work and research were to just dis‐
appear, I think it would be I've been working to push this society towards Ronald Koest's magical world
of zero transaction costs. So I'm using identity and AI and every tool at my disposal to reduce the cost of
doing commercial transactions to bring shared economic prosperity. If it were to go away, I think we'd be
stuck with centralized databases, centralized processes that don't scale and are not they're beholden to
the existing gatekeepers and it does not allow us to push forward as a society. Thank you.

Applying KERI to General
Evidence Problems
Daniel Hardman
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · TALKS · 39:53
35
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Okidoke. I suppose everybody's pretty tired at this point, but I hope that I can keep your attention
and keep you awake through this presentation. This is about Dossiers. I think that this is actually
one of the more profound topics that this community could be .., thought leaders and world ex‐
perts on there's a lot of untapped potential in this and I don't think we realize how cool that potential is.
So, I hope I can convince you and get you guys excited about this.
This topic represents kind of the synthesis of a bunch of stuﬀ that I've been working on in the telco
space, but it really is not a telco topic. I remember when I first heard Sam talk about ACDCs circa 2019.
He came to a Trust over IP working group meeting and started talking about ACDC theory and Sam men‐
tioned This phrase that ACDCs are a verifiable data graph and at the time I thought oh that's cool and it
didn't really like impinge on my consciousness that deeply but I have since come to feel that's actually
quite a profound statement and there's more to it than we give it credit for and we could use this to solve
some problems that people can't solve elsewhere.
So let me describe the problem as I encountered it in telco. I came to telco from a background in veri‐
fiable credentials. And in verifiable credentials the assumption is that you prove something to a verifier
and you the party who is proving something are having a direct interaction with the verifier.
Right? the verifier sends you a proof request or something and the proof request arrives at your wal‐
let and you get you know it gets your attention and you make a decision and then the proof flows back
the other way. Well, guess what? That does not work in telco, at least in the use cases I was exploring,
because for one thing, we wanted it to be the case that the party that emitted a communication and
signed something Could have that thing that they're trying to prove verified all along the way.
Not only that, the party on the other side doesn't have a channel to go back yet. So we needed basical‐
ly one-way multiple-party verification and not only that I needed verification to be possible Completely
out of the out of band as well like having an auditor or a regulator pull a transcript of the conversation
and verify it a day later a year later or whatever to see if we were taking the right actions.
And again that's not really what verifiable credentials are built to do. This is all setting aside the more
fundamental problems which are, you know, verifiable credentials have mutable schemas and all those
other things. But anyway, if you set all that aside, I still couldn't solve the problem, right?
The other thing that became very obvious is that we needed a much richer evidence model than the
typical verifiable credential would provide. Almost 100% of the verifiable credential workflows that you
see demoed or discussed anywhere out in the community are single credential workflows: "Show me your
driver's license" or "show me some subset of the driver's license," right? Or "show me your passport" or
"show me your COVID thing" or whatever. But there are all kinds of scenarios in real life every day that
call for very rich collections of evidence.
I listed a few up here and I put them under kind of headings because I see these patterns showing up.
So, there's all kinds of processes in the real world that are evidenceoriented that deal with applications,
right? You're going to apply for a grant.
You're going to apply to go to college. You're going to submit a regulatory filing. You're going to apply
for a job. All of these are processes. They're typically multi-step processes where a large body of evidence
has to be assembled and submitted.
36
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
That's not the same pattern as this nice tidy request response Of verifiable credentials. There's also
research activities that happen. you're conducting a scientific study and you want to compile a whole
bunch of evidence and if you'd like to solve the problems that we have in the world of academic research
with a nonreproducibility, you'd like that evidence that you compile to be verifiable, right?
Investigative journalism, Criminal investigations, all of these fall into this category as well. And no‐
tice here, the evidence that you're putting in a lot of these things accretes over time. It's not just evidence
that you just have in one issuance event and then you're done.
When you're trying to document something, maybe you're trying to prove Some things about the rea‐
sons why your company was valued a particular way to regulators or investors, or you're trying to capture
the results of a forensic accounting audit. Or you're trying to submit documents for a court case, A court
filing. All of these are cases where evidence is quite important and verifiability is quite important.
But the simple trust triangle that we talk about in the verifiable credentials world is really quite dif‐
ferent from what the patterns of behavior are. Another thing that became really clear to me is in real‐
world hardcore ambitious use cases.
Lifespans of evidence, is a big deal. It's surprising .., now that I've become sensitized to it, it's almost
shocking to me that this is never said, but you'll hear somebody analyzing you know can we prove 'xyz'
with let's lsay with certificates or with credentials or whatever and they'll go through a whole deep analy‐
sis about whether it can be proved and they'll completely omit the fact that they've got four diﬀerent
kinds of evidence that they're trying to prove with. And the evidence has diﬀerent lifespans, right? So,
one piece of evidence that they're building from has a year-long lifespan.
Another piece of evidence has a 20 minute lifespan. And when you combine those two, what's the
combined lifespan of the thing? 20 minutes. But nobody ever analyzes it that way. And so, they're the
very naive views about lifespan of evidence.
And you know, I just kind of randomly picked it: Three diﬀerent categories. You could group evidence
into very diﬀerent lifespan categories than these ones I picked. But the point is we need to start thinking
about the lifespan of evidence a little bit more carefully. And if we do, we're going to realize that the sim‐
ple way that we've been trying to solve certain problems is really quite inadequate.
Another thing is that the purpose of the diﬀerent constituent pieces of evidence that you might as‐
semble in a collection could be quite diﬀerent and have very diﬀerent assumptions. You don't see this
when you're only doing kind of .., it's dismissive for me to say "Mickey Mouse" demonstrations, but when
you're doing demonstrations are really quite simplistic and you're using one credential and you're saying I
can either show you all of my driver's license or part of it, you don't realize this, but as soon as you start
assembling Evidence to solve a real world problem, you find that the diﬀerent kinds of evidence that
you're grouping together introduce some interesting complexity. So in this particular slide, I'm imagining
what's the evidence that a loan oﬃcer has to assemble to get you qualified for a mortgage. Okay? And I
just profiled four diﬀerent kinds. So one piece of evidence that a loan oﬃcer has to assemble is your last
three paystubs.
Right? Now, how those last three pay stubs are stubs are verified is pretty diﬀerent from some of the
other things up here. And how long those paystubs can be useful is diﬀerent from some of the other
things up here and so forth. So, that's the last three paystubs. And the purpose of putting it together in
this collection is, you're trying to prove that the person has a certain kind of income. And that means (my
37
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
red text there) these have to be three consecutive paystubs and they have to be recent and you know all
these other rules apply to that right then how about proof of employment that's a whole diﬀerent kind of
evidence and proof of employment is probably you want quite recent proof of employment maybe a dif‐
ferent recency standard than you have to meet with the paystubs and the kind of credential you're using
to prove this is quite diﬀerent as well and it has a diﬀerent issuer, right?
How about the credit report? Yet another issuer, yet another standard, yet another governance, and so
forth. If you're really trying to solve a serious use case with rich evidence, what you have to model about
that evidence and prove about that evidence to achieve what Sam said years ago about, you know, a verifi‐
able data graph is really quite sophisticated.
It's not as simple as "Oh, show somebody a credential" and you're done. You also kind of need to
know what's the purpose of the collection of the evidence you're putting together. You're not just stuﬃng
evidence in a drawer somewhere so you can call upon it. In the case of the loan, going back to that, all of
these pieces of evidence that I was talking about are going to be evaluated at funding.
That's the time of the evaluation or the verification. Now, actually, in the case of a mortgage, they're
pre-evaluated once or twice before that and re-evaluated at funding. After funding, they don't get evaluat‐
ed anymore. So, you have to kind of know when you're building up a collection of evidence what you're
building it up for and when you plan to have it be verified.
Ideally, you want to be able to verify it forever, but in the context of the event that was important. So,
you're never going to ask the question, would I give somebody this loan today, a year after they got their
mortgage, but you might go back a year after they got their mortgage, and say, were they qualified for this
loan when they got it, which is a diﬀerent question.
As we wrestled with issues like this, and I used the mortgage example here just so that you wouldn't
think I'm only thinking about telco, but you know, again, it was telco that was forcing me to think about
this. People were asking, well, how can I prove and then fill in the blank with a bunch of telco related
questions and I was grappling with all of these issues. And this is where I kind of ended up. And I feel
like I kind of came back to "Oh, KERI has a superpower that I wasn't giving enough credit to before."
So first of all, we started calling this construct of a bunch of evidence that you've assembled a Dossier,
for lack of a better word, seem to resonate with people. I could have called it a file, but that's so generic
that I just picked dossier.
You notice that I'm using these icons here to represent the fact that it's a collection of diﬀerent evi‐
dence types and you have some kind of a process that feeds all this evidence in and then you go through
this curation process. You basically get the evidence to the point where you like it. This is like going to
the loan oﬃcer and saying, "Okay, I've got all my documents in. Now you can evaluate and decide
whether to give me a loan." Right?
Now you actually might have more than one of those points. You might evaluate it one time or seven
times. Or you could evaluate it for a very long time to come. But you need to know what your plans are
about that. The idea here is that this dossier is intended to be shown to somebody but not presented in
the sense of a verifiable presentation.
38
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
You don't have to prove that you are the issuee of everything in the dossier. Let's say it's an investiga‐
tive journalism dossier. you're not proving entitlements with this thing but you have to prove that all of
these interviews that happened with certain sources really took place or whatever. So, it's not the simple
model that you get with verifiable credentials where you're saying here's the thing and I'm proving it for
myself on behalf of myself and I'm entitled to something.
Once you have a dossier, this is the other thing that I became very comfortable with in the telco con‐
text. The world's telco systems are built to move very small amounts of metadata. So let's take the case of
SMS. When you send an SMS message, in theory, you're sending 140 characters or less.
Nowadays, phones will send stuﬀ over RCS instead of SMS. And so you can exceed that limit. But the
original S SNPP protocol wanted to move 140 character or less things. And now imagine that you were
trying to move the equivalent of let's say a verifiable credential or even an ACDC in the headers of that.
You have headers that are like five times as big as the message itself. That's not very eﬀective. What
we can do though, is we can derive, and this is part of KERI's superpower, we can derive from a dossier. If
it's really a verifiable data graph, that means it has a SAID.
Which means, I can just send the SAID in this really light form and somebody on the other side who
understands that it's a SAID can say, "Oh, I bet there's a whole bunch of data behind that." And go fetch it
and it works.
And we can make that a little bit more convenient by turning the set into a OOBI. Basically now I can
take something and here I wrote 'token,' this is a word that I don't like a lot, I kind of roll my eyes when
people talk about security and tokens. But in some contexts that's the way security is enforced. So in the
case of voice calls, for example, every VOIP system in the world carries a JWT with every .., well it can,
it's built to carry a JWT with every SIP invite. So when you pull out your phone and you dial a phone
number and you hit the dial button, your phone is causing a SIP invite, which is a small message to flow
over the world, the internet. And typically it uses the real internet for parts of the transmission and then
it flows over specialized telco circuits for other parts of the conversation. But anyway, somehow your SIP
invite ends up on the other side and that's when the phone starts ringing is because the SIP invite arrived
at the other location.
That SIP invite looks very much like an HTTP request and it has headers. The SIP protocol and the
HTTP protocol are kissing cousins. They look so similar. They even to the point where 400 range errors
in SIP are 400 range errors in HTTP and everything - Intentionally?
Yeah, they very deliberately copied HTTP when they built SIP. So all that you're doing really with that
infrastructure, if you want to decorate your call with evidence, is you're putting a header .., and think of it
like an HTTP header, It's almost identical. It says identity colon space and then a bunch of stuﬀ that's B64
encoded. And that B64 encoding is that B64 encoding is the JWT that I'm talking about.
And that JWT can have a field in it, a claim that is the URL to your evidence. That's basically all we're
doing. But this same technique can be applied well outside of telco. And that's why I'm interested in this.
So you can derive any number of tokens that keep referring back to the same piece of evidence. And that's
what we're doing over and over again when you make phone calls. If you're Microsoft and you're making
outbound calls, every outbound call that you make carries a hyperlink back to the same evidence, but a
new signature, right?

So what does a dossier actually look like? For those of you who are ACDC people, this is a trivially sim‐

You know what those mean, attributes and edges. All that a dossier is really is a particular kind of

like who built and assembled this thing when was it assembled under what governance rules was it as‐

that I submitted is down in the edges.
The edges just point to another ACDC. That's right. The edges just point to another ACDC. And this
is where we get verifiable data graphs. Yeah. There's a lot of diﬀerent kinds of evidence that you might

Anyway, so these two techniques, embedding and wrapping are what I'm contrasting on this slide.
The idea with embedding is you have data that's not credential oriented, but it's still data that's impor‐
tant. Scientific readings you know, all these other examples I went through. So, you want your assembly
of evidence to reference it.

your accident report.
Wrapping is relevant when the thing that you want to put in your verifiable data graph is another
form of verifiable data, but it's what I would call foreign to the ACDC world. So if you have an X.509 cer‐

you want to put it in your data graph, you can do that. The tech now you can do it by doing an embed‐

this wrapping. What you're essentially doing is you're verifying the evidence with its native stack and
then you're issuing a new ACDC that says on such and such a date I so and so verified this credential and
it was legit on that date and you're making an explicit claim one way or the other about whether you take
any responsibility for revocation status.

All of us have been impacted by scamming in telco. Usually a day doesn't go by that I don't get some
kind of a message. This morning it was somebody running for Congress in some state that I've never
lived in telling me how important it was that I donate $3 to his campaign. But all of us get these kinds of
things. And many of us who work in cyber security sensitive areas are pretty well aware of how scammy
and fraudulent a lot of it is.
Even people who don't know anything about tech know that this is scammy and and full of fraud to
the point where this is the answer they're coming up with. Just don't answer your phone, right? Or just
stop sending texts.
That's actually a pretty bad indictment of a technical infrastructure when the best advice we can give
is just stop using it. But that's kind of the state of aﬀairs right now. And that constitutes an existential
threat for an entire market vertical.
There are billion-dollar companies that make their money oﬀ of phone calls or make their money oﬀ
of texting. And so if you have entire influential voices, segments in an industry that are saying just stop
using that tech entirely, there's panic going on.
Microsoft is an interesting example of this and so I'm going to use them because I know some details
about their world. One of the common scams is maybe you've had a grandparent who says, "Oh, I got a
call from Microsoft tech support."
The reason that scam is dangerous is because Microsoft does have a tech support staﬀ and they do
sometimes call people. They don't spontaneously call people, which is the way the scam usually works.
But if a person calls Microsoft tech support and they get disconnected, Or if they call and they have the
end of the current conversation, but there needs to be follow-up, Microsoft does call people.
The problem is people don't want to answer. And that's a serious pain point for Microsoft. And regu‐
lators are getting involved in this now because Microsoft has another characteristic that's kind of compli‐
cated that would be true of anybody who's a giant global conglomerate, which is they put their people in
the most strategic places they can for cost reasons and so forth. So particular case in point in the UK
Microsoft has plenty of customers that they interact with but Microsoft doesn't put their call center staﬀ
in the UK. They put their call center staﬀ in Bulgaria or some other lowcost center that can you know
make a cheap call into the UK. But Ofcom, which is the equivalent of the FCC in the UK has some prob‐
lems with that because they don't want a phone call to be claiming that it's coming from the UK and from
Microsoft when it's coming from Bulgaria.
And so they have said, you may not attribute this phone call to a UK number unless you can prove it.
You have to prove that it's really coming from Microsoft and this is creating a lot of pain for Microsoft. So
what Microsoft wants is a transformation of the customer experience from the phone on the left to the
phone on the right. They'd like it to be the case that when the phone rings, it shows the Microsoft logo. It
shows the Microsoft name and it shows why they're calling. Oh, they're calling because they're customer
support representative and they want to be able to prove it.

What you will see looks like a branded call. However, it is a little bit diﬀerent. With OVC, the infor‐

They prove that Microsoft has the right to the telephone number that is being displayed, that it is
Microsoft Corporation that is calling you, linked back to the Axel legal entity responsible for the call us‐

Microsoft could use these three credentials to originate calls themselves, but there is a fourth creden‐

project, we've worked with Infobip, Twilio, and Vonage as platforms that could be used to sign traﬃc on

You can see here the name, telephone number and brand along with a verified tick are displayed.
These are displayed based on a successful verification of the evidence provided in the communication.
What we'll do next is have a look at the governance model that underpins this evidence.
One of the biggest challenges with global verifiable communications is ensuring there is thorough
and consistent vetting of original parties that can work in many diﬀerent circumstances. OVC has a rich
governance model and provable chain of evidence to support this. It starts with a local governance au‐
thority that sets the vetting standards for OVC.
We next have policy administrators who appointed by the government's authority to apply these vet‐
ting standards in a given jurisdiction. It's important as there are many diﬀerences of regulations and vet‐
ting processes in diﬀerent countries. They're responsible for certifying vetting agents to be able to vet in
these jurisdictions for diﬀerent communications channels.
Once credentials been issued to vetting agents, they're able to vet enterprises in specific regions and
for specific communications channels. In the trial, we've had brand numerical and [inaudible] enterpris‐
es. The vetting agents verify enterprises to prove rights to telephone numbers, that they are the legal en‐
47
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
tity they say they are, and that they have the rights to use the brand, and then issue the enterprises with
appropriate credentials. This process can support support both small businesses and large corporations.
It's important to note that these credentials are chained all the way back to the governance authority
providing a provable chain of trust. It is also means that it's possible for credentials to be revoked at any
point in the chain.
So if for example a vetter does not follow the rules and vets bad actors, it's possible for a policy ad‐
ministrator to revoke their credential and have a cascade impact on the credential set issue. The creden‐
tials are held in wallets by enterprises to whom they have been issued. While the focus of the demonstra‐
tion has been on voice, these credentials can also prove other communication channels such as RCS, SMS
or APIs. The final credential which can be created if needed is a delegated signed credential which we dis‐
cussed earlier.
What we'll look at now is how the evidence looks for a real call. First, we're going to make another
call, but this time from another one. One of the credentials has been removed by Microsoft. We will call
the same number.
As you can see, in this instance, the call fails. This is because the transit operator verifying the net‐
work attempted to verify the call and found that one of the credentials had been revoked and therefore
rejected the call.
We will now go and look at the evidence detail for this call. We can see the call here. Evidence detail
records refer back to the evidence and provide a permanent record of the evidence accompanying the call.
We can see the three credentials here.
The identity credential, the TN credential, and the brand credential. And we can see here that the TN
credential has been revoked. We can also look into the evidence in more detail and see who has vetted
Microsoft. In this case, it was Aegis and even how they gained their authority to be a vetter. We can also
then see the policy administrator and the government's authority for the trial.
These were just placeholders. We'll now move on to look at the actual call flow for the call that you've
seen. We started with a set of credentials that have been issued to the enterprise and delegated to an
originator so they can make calls on their behalf.
When the call is made, the originator calls out to a signing service from Provenant, the technology
provider for this project. The service returns a compact passport, a STIR passport and also replicates this
passport from out-of-band cache. The passport is not a shaken passport but a verifiable voice protocol
passport.
Once signed, the originating party sends the phone onto the PSTN. Here it is received by transit oper‐
ator Wavecrest, it takes the passport and uses a verification service to verify the pool. Verification can
happen anywhere in the call flow. The ideal scenario is that verification takes place in the terminating
network. In the case of the good call, Wavecrest will pass this call to the terminating operator. And in the
case of a bad call, Wavecrest rejects the call.
When the call arrives on the handset, it does a look up to an out-of-bound passport cache. If a pass‐
port is found, a verification again takes place and if successful, the result is returned to the handset. The
outcome of the verification is then displayed as the phone rings.
48
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
In our trial, we relied on verification on the handset using a modified version of the standard Android
dialer provided by Google for this project and a lookup was to a public out-of-band service. In an ideal
scenario, the verification would take place in the terminating operator's network with the cryptographic
evidence being passed directly to the handset or to the out-of-band service to be deployed in the opera‐
tor's network.

JWT. And I'm going to show you how little I really mean when I say a little bit. It's actually remarkable
how rich we can make this with a very tiny addition. And because the world systems already know how

they're going to other places. So anyway, there's some real live in production stuﬀ by companies that you
know that are doing this and the GSMA completed this OVC project in March, just a month ago. So now
they've announced that they're going to have some follow-on projects. One of them is going to focus on
governance.
And this gets to the whole LEI and vLEI topic. During the proof of concept stuﬀ that we did, we were
using an LEI to identify the companies and everybody agreed that was vital. And so now they're looking
to extend that and fit into the vLEI family.
That's part of the follow-on governance work. The other thing is that they explicitly want to make this
technology usable in multiple channels. So the channel that we focused on in this first experience was
voice calls. But they care very much since the world's mobile technology providers. They care about tex‐
ting just as much as they care about phone calls. They care about rich chat and how iMessage works and
bunch of things like that. And so when I say multi-channel, that's what I'm talking about. Now, I never
explained the acronym OVC. So, let me just spend a minute. OVC originally meant Open Verifiable
Calling.
But during this foundry project, people said, "Well, wait a minute. There's no reason why we couldn't
take this evidence that we've built into these other channels." Right? So OVC has now become in the
minds and in the communications that go on in GSMA channels like their mailing list and the meetings
that they have. They've started calling it Open Verifiable Communication and that's what OVC now
means with this check mark. They're explicitly pursuing communication as a general goal, not just call‐
ing. So I have a little bit more information about that in a second.
Sam made a point in his presentation that we don't want to have a single vendor solution. Provenant
was kind of the enabler of this whole thing. And of course Provenant is a for-profit company and we hope
we can sell lots of people on what we've built. But it's important for it to be open and we believe in that.
So here's some kind of proof points about how open verifiable calling and the verifiable voice protocol
work and thank you to this is a shout out to the Trust over IP folks who've let us build a Dossier specifi‐

this whole concept and Also to the folks over at IETF that we're working with.

Okay. Let me talk a little bit since this is a more geeky audience. It's not just pure business people. I
want to talk about what's going on underneath here. When we looked at what we had to build, these are
the kinds of requirements that we felt we were getting from the business side and the regulatory side.
And I'm not going to read all of them out loud, but I hope when you look at that you say, "Well, that's a
pretty ambitious list because it felt kind of overwhelming to us." building half of those would be pretty
easy, but when you start having to build all of those things in or address all those requirements, as Sam
said, you get into a very hard trade space where you're making trade-oﬀs and some of them are hard. And
we didn't want to trade away any of these requirements. And because we're here at a KERI conference, I
will just say I don't believe we could have built this with any other technology. There were a number of
reasons why you end up at a dead end if you try to solve it any other way.
So without belaboring that, let me say also it was important to identify some things we did not have
to do. And this is important because there are calling solutions that deliver significant value that have
some of these requirements and we were not asked to address some of these. The most interesting one
I'll just mention the bottom one here.
Somebody said, "Oh, well, this is all well and good, but how do I know that an AI is not, you know,
sending me words and pretending that it's coming from another person?" And the answer is we're not
trying to solve that problem.
The reason we're not trying to solve that problem is because for us, the issue is about accountability.
If Microsoft connects to you, then it's on Microsoft if they once they're connected with you and you know
you're really dealing with Microsoft who made the call. If Microsoft then sticks an AI on the call, that's
their problem and your problem, but it's not the problem of the technology that connected you and
proved that Microsoft was accountable in the first place. So, it's about accountability.
Not about whether it's human or not. However, we could certainly prove that the party who dialed
you was an actual human being versus an AI if we needed to. And I'll show you where that fits if that be‐
came interesting. So, these are some of the things that were the places where maybe the trade-oﬀs mani‐
fest and it would be hard to address some of these requirements.
The top one is the maybe the biggest. Okay, how do you do all of this but you do it with a commodity
SIM card over networks that haven't been modified. If you can do that, that's a big deal. Anyway, there
another one, the third one there, I just want to mention, it's possible to prove things between a caller and
a callee using classic trust triangle that we've talked about for years using verifiable credentials.
However, In that kind of a model, the parties have to know who they're talking to in a sense before
you can prove because basically you have to have a connection with the other party in some kind of an
out-of-band way before you can then prove with your credentials. And the specific characteristic we need‐
ed for this solution was that a stranger could call you. You haven't had any prior knowledge about who
these parties might be that are reaching out to you.
Okay. What does this look like? If I gave you a really high-level architecture diagram, it might look
like this. You have a party like Acme Corporation that wants to make a call. Sometime near when the call
originates like right at the same time or within milliseconds afterwards if they're putting traﬃc on a net‐
work and they're were you using like a cloud provider or whatever as the call originates, a signing event
occurs the signing that we're talking about is a signing of the JWT.
51
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
And the JWT is this thing that's called a passport That term passport is a term of art in the IETF spec‐
ification for these JWTs. So this passport contains .., and the blue with the underscore here is supposed
to suggest this to you, contains a hyperlink to what we're calling a dossier.
The dossier is the collection of rich evidence. So all we're adding to this passport that it didn't have
before is a field that has a hyperlink in it. The relationship between the signer of the passport and the
passport itself is crucial and we could talk about that oﬄine. I probably won't go into it in my talk that
much. Suﬃce it to say that you have to prove that the party signing has some relationship of delegated
authority with the party that is originating the call. It could be the one in the same party, but it doesn't
have to be. And that's important because you have to support things like call centers That are other busi‐
nesses. The call center is not Microsoft and a regulator needs to be able to tell those two parties apart.
But you have to be able to prove that the call center is acting on Microsoft's behalf. They can't just assert
it.
There's a whole story about how evidence is built and I'm not going to go into it in detail, but essen‐
tially you start out with a cryptographic identifier for the company and a control mechanism. Those two
things go together. And once you have those two, then you build an identity credential. And for those of
you who know how vLEIs work, this is straight out of Karla's thinking.
It's exactly how vLEIs are built. Basically steps one through four are the journey of getting a vLEI.
And then you add to that a bunch of other credentials. Importantly, the credentials are issued by diﬀerent
parties. So you have a collection of issuers.
They all have their own revocation criteria and their own revocation SLAs. So we can't put all of this
in a single credential. And then you bundle all the credentials together in a Dossier. And the Dossier in‐
cludes a last thing which has to be a credential issued by the actual company that we're talking about de‐
claring their intentions to make this kind of traﬃc flow.
And then you can use it in ongoing use. Now some of you may be wondering, here we are at a KERI
conference, can this technique be used without ACDCs without KERI. No, it can't. You can't build this
whole solution but you can interoperate with other kinds of credentials quite nicely. So if a company has
a credential and the format of this credential is a format that's required by the EU to prove something
about the company and they want to use that to prove one of the logical propositions in the dossier, you
can do that. You could accept a credential that is a W3C verifiable credential. You can accept an SD jot.
You can accept a signed PDF if you want as evidence, but you have to wrap it somehow because unless
you can put it in a verifiable data graph and you can reason about it in a particular way, the solution falls
apart. You can't end up generating that view that I was showing you in the evidence that has the badges
and stuﬀ like that. So, there's two basic ways that you can deal with that. You can either use wrapped
mode, which is where I take a foreign piece of evidence and put an ACDC around it, and literally the
ACDC has the content of the other piece of evidence right inside it.
And it's a reissuance essentially. So, you have an issuer of an ACDC that's saying, "I looked at this
other piece of evidence and I used its native technology, whatever it is, W3C verifiable credential stack or
something, and I verified it on date X."
And so now I'm issuing an ACDC that says on date X, this credential was verified and it was true.
That's wrapped mode. And you can see that wrapped mode has some good things about it, and some bad
things about it. The good things are that a party on the other end doesn't have to know about any other
52
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
stacks or technology types. It only has to verify ACDCs.
But you have a revocation issue. Does the party who's doing the reissuance sign up to also track the
revocation? To be determined, right? The other one is you can have embedded mode and in that mode
the party that's creating the ACDC doesn't take a lot of responsibility. They basically say here's an ACDC
envelope around data but what it means is up to you to determine and so you have to have a stack that
can look and understand this other evidence format and decide whether it agrees with the guarantees
that it provides. I don't think that embedded mode is a good idea because it fragments the verification
space.
But anyway, enough about that. I won't go into great detail on this diagram. I could talk about it for a
very long time, but I'll simply say this. The way that passports in STIR were conceived of is that a pass‐
port was supposed to include an X5U field that was a hyperlink to the certificate of the signer of the
passport.
Okay. And when you went and looked up that signer certificate, you would see that the certificate in‐
cluded proof of a cryptographic identity, that would be their public key and it included information about
the attributes of this party. So that'd be like the common name of the company and their organizational
unit or you know all that other stuﬀ. What we've done is we've split that apart and said information
about identity and information about attributes need to be handled diﬀerently because identity can be
permanent.
On this model. How often do you have to replace this? All the stinking time? I don't know exactly
what number you're going to come up with, but I guarantee it's a number I don't like. Right? If it's once a
month or once every three months or once a week, SHAKEN in the United States mandated by the FCC
calls for three-month rotations in their guidance. I think they enforce a higher number than that, but
okay. Anyway they're only doing it right now. The certificates that SHAKEN use are only for big compa‐
nies like Twilio and whatever.
They're not for the millions of little companies around the world. So, they said, "Well, let's take this
thing and we'll start using certificates on all the little companies around the world." Can you imagine if
every little company around the world has to replace proof of who they are? Let's say there's 30 million
businesses in the United States alone. And so you're going to be maintaining a fabric of 30 million certifi‐
cates that rotate once a month.
It's completely impractical. And so here, how often do we rotate this? Zero times. I really love that
answer. You can rotate the keys as often as you want, but I don't have to replace the identifier ever. And I
can't tell you how profound the diﬀerence in cost is and the diﬀerence in infrastructure lift when you
don't have to replace anything. It's huge.
Now, we do have to change attributes, but we only have to change them as often as the attributes
change, right? If your company doesn't rename itself, you don't need a new proof of what its name is.
And if it doesn't lose the ability to use a logo in a particular jurisdiction, you don't need to replace that ei‐
ther. So again, the maintenance is dramatically diﬀerent.
This is how it actually looks on the wire today. On the left is a standard SHAKEN passport. This is
what actually flows over the wire right now. And the reason that we could deploy this with no changes in
infrastructure is because this is how diﬀerent it looks over here.

KERI Conference April 2026 Videos

– Well, the URL is an OOBI. So it's not a generic URL, meaning that when you go there, you must see
a signed sequence of events that proves the its evolution from its origin moment, its inception moment.
So you could have somebody who tries to replace the content at that location, but if they did, they would
have to come up with content that still evolved from scratch and was still related to the identifier in

bunch of monkeying with that. And interestingly enough, this is one of the debates that I've been having
in the STIR working group at IETF because people said, "Oh, this is a great idea. We'll put a hyperlink in
there. We'll just accept any hyperlink."
And I said, "No, that's not quite a good idea." For the very concern that you're talking about, we'll see
if I can convince them. They might just modify the spec and say any hyperlink will do. In which case… –

the call might have somebody call them back, Or it call their watchers and witnesses back. But these are
entities that they control and have chosen. So it's not calling home or phoning home to a central registry.

Daniel-Hardman-Interview
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · INTERVIEWS · 01:16
56
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
My name is Daniel Hardman and I'm the CTO of Provenant, which is a small startup in the telco in‐
dustry. My presentation was on open verifiable calling. That acronym OVC is changing into open
verifiable communication and it's all about proving who is making some kind of a communication
flow to somebody else. So the big thing that we're doing that I think is crucial is we're providing evidence
for the first time about who is involved in communication. Up until now, most eﬀorts to eliminate fraud
have basically proved somebody's opinion, but now we're actually able to prove hard facts about the par‐
ties involved. I think without the OVC initiative, the entire telco industry is missing an important insight
and an opportunity. Fraud is driving down the usefulness of all of our communication technologies and
unless they come up with an answer, people are going to stop answering their phones. They're going to
stop texting and we need to have strong evidence so that these technologies remain viable and deliver
real value.!

We have been developing a web wallet for KERI essentially just as you know more wallets more
platforms better. Initially Sam [Smith] set out these goals where we wanted to create a fully web
native wallet because currently the Locksmith application is a desktop application.
Core wallet, we wanted it to run entirely in the browser with no like server or anything running
KERIpy on the back end. Which, proved to be a challenge in fact. Again, we wanted everything to happen
in the browser, so you can have all of your keys locally created and stored on your device. Don't have to
worry about them being stored on some server. Then we could just send you this application and you
would get your in-browser wallet and we also wanted it to be persistent because that would be a little bit
useless if it wasn't.
Some challenges: Running Python in a WebAssembly was the first big hurdle. because currently
Python is run on CPython which is a C-written interpreter. The cryptography had to work in the browser
and this basically meant that KERIpy couldn't drag LMDB into the web path because LMDB relies on
MMAP which is not supported in WebAssembly.
And, again, we want this to be not a one tab, one reload type of wallet. We want it to actually be like
a production grade wallet that we can deploy where you can just take the files, serve them yourself, even
if you wanted to, and be able to have your own identifiers that you can manage from a browser and not
have to download an application on specific architectures. So getting Python into the browser was a in‐
teresting challenge. We found Piodide which is essentially the Python interpreter compiled to
WebAssembly which allows it to run in the browser which means that you can then run any Python na‐
tive packages and for all the non-Python native packages that was a medium challenge And then we have
PyScript as like the browser bridge so you can launch oﬀ web workers in the browser and it interfaces
with like the browser database so you can have again that persistent storage which is exactly what I put
on my slides.
To do this we had to again go through the dependencies of KERIpy get them to work in the browser
which started with pysodium which is a python wrapper for Libsodium which is a C library. So I had to
take Libsodium compile it to target WebAssembly and then I changed some of the code in Pysodium so
that it loaded the packaged Libsodium that I created, called it pychloride because it's like you know sodi‐
umchloride… that was Sam's idea.
I'll give credit where credit due. Blake3 is written in Rust. I had to compile this WebAssembly and
also in an attempt to get ready for future proofing we took this pyoqs which is an open quantum secure
safety library for key creation and made it WebAssembly compatible. So that will be coming in the future
hopefully before quantum computers.
So in KERIpy itself we had to make it WebAssembly safe, which meant creating a second database
backing which used indexedDB which runs in the browser. We created 'webdber' instead of 'lmdber'
which means we're no longer relying on the MMAP LMDB And we also had to make it so that the im‐
ports don't randomly import lmdb so that it works in the browser. And again, this webdber allowed us to
create like a persistent storage in the browser that can survive when you press reload on accident and
then you lose all your keys because that would suck.

Signify Security Architecture
Fergal O'Conner
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · TALKS · 42:28
