KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So hello everyone and welcome. My name is Fergal O'Connor. I'm the identity solutions lead architect
at the Cardano Foundation. I've been there for the last four years now helping out build out our
open-source identity solution known as Veridian.
Last year we launched the first mobile wallet in the KERI/ACDC ecosystem to production. This is a
production-grade mobile identity wallet. It's gone through security auditing and pentesting. And in gen‐
eral we put a lot of eﬀort into the user experience of this wallet and how we could bring KERI and ACDC
to the masses and make it more accessible.
The wallet itself is based on KERIA and Signify-ts. And throughout the years, we've put a lot of eﬀort
into contributing to KERIA and Signify-ts in the open source community. Particularly because we were
trying to bring in this extra user experience.
There was kind of gaps in the existing software that was required to deliver that user experience we
wanted. And also some kind of security edge cases kind of operationally that we had to add in, to align
with our pentesting.
Today I'm going to be going over kind of the security architecture of KERIA and Signify-ts. This is in
the advanced track so we'll be quite technical. I think it's generally okay if you have kind of a background
in into KERI but some parts might be a bit low level.
Now, the first tagline I want to start with is: KERIA is a cloud agent In the KERI ecosystem. And kind
of in line with everything that Sam [Smith, red.] was speaking about this morning, I put the tagline here
of “Agent in the cloud, controller at the edge”. So just because you're using a cloud agent doesn't mean
that you give up control of your keys.
So if you have a mobile wallet which is using a cloud agent based on KERIA your keys will always do
the signing at the edge on your phone. And I also took this snippet directly from the KERI specification.
Sam defined that a controller application needs to do five functions.
This is keypair generation and storage, key event generation and signing and key event validation. Of
course this is at the lowest level of KERI. It doesn't talk about higher level protocols such as ACDCs or
OOBIs or IPEX and so forth. But if you have that controller application with those five functionalities.
You can, if you wish, split it across two kind of software deployments of a controller application on the
left and a controller agent on the right. So KERI is your controller agent. The split here is that your con‐
troller application, say your mobile wallet will have to do the keypair generation, key event generation
and signing. And then your controller agent in the cloud can do keypair storage and key event validation.
Now the keypair storage of course has to be encrypted because your agent cannot access those keys be‐
cause that would break everything. And I think also with this diagram you could put the keypair storage
in either side that works in the setup of the deployment.
But critically the key event validation is pushed to the agent in the cloud. Okay. So particularly when
you start adding in other protocols such as ACDCs and so forth, you're pushing all of this complex vali‐
dation logic that must be spec compliant and so forth to the cloud and abstracting away the details in a
way that your controller applications can be a bit more lightweight at the edge. All you have to do is sign
some events, essentially, and not do all of the complex validation.
66
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Okay. So today we're going to cover kind of three areas. First and foremost the kind of service archi‐
tecture of KERIA and Signify How it works in general. Then we'll delve a bit deeper into the trust rela‐
tionship between the cloud and edge. What keys live where you know what must be signed to establish
this trust relationship and so forth. And then finally to wrap it all up we'll see and explore how to use
KERI to interact with the rest of the world once you set this up.
So on the service architecture… As an overview before we get into the diagrams: KERIA is of course a
KERI agent in the cloud. It's based on KERIPy directly. So is the Python service that uses KERIPy as a di‐
rect dependency. Most of the complex validation is from KERI core. It's essentially an orchestration unit
wrapped around it. It's a multi- agent service. So it allows you to provision many agents for many con‐
trollers and kind of to summarize it's a set of HTTP endpoints essentially for controlling these agents. So
provisioning them utilizing them and to interact with the rest of the world.
Signify on the other hand is your edge client library. This is what your controller application would
use. Again the main idea here is to sign at the edge. I believe in the early days people were going to call
Signify 'KATE': “keys at the edge”. They stuck with Signify however. But it's essentially just basically like
an API wrapper around KERIA. Except they can do this key event generation and signing as well.
So it understands bits of CESR and so forth. The relationship is: for every edge client there is one
cloud agent, a direct one-to-one mapping. And it's available in multiple languages. So within the
WebOfTrust the two complete implementations are Signify-ts in TypeScript and SignifyPy. I don't believe
SignifyPy is used in production but Signify-ts is used by most people in this ecosystem, to be honest, that
aren't building directly on KERIPy. In the Cardano Foundation we've also built a full Java implementation
of Signify. It's fully feature complete. That's actually been used internally at the Cardano Foundation and
a number of other projects that we're working on which have actually gone to production.
And in general one nice thing about Signify is that right now it gives you a higher level API that is
more accessible to actually utilize KERI. So if you were to build directly on KERIPy there's a steep learn‐
ing curve to understand how it works internally. And it would be very easy to go wrong. Whereas with
Signify the API is a bit more like: create this identifier, create this ACDC registry, issue this credential.
I think having that higher-level API also brings much more accessibility to a wider audience for adop‐
tion and because it's available in multiple (programming, red) languages that also makes things easier.
I'm not going to say it's super simple to add new languages for Signify but it's much more trivial than
porting KERIPy to various languages.
So if we to look at this overview diagram of KERI is here in the center as I mentioned it's a multi-
agent service. So we have agents A, B and C. Each agent has its own fully isolated database. These data‐
bases are mostly provisioned by KERIPy. They're a set of LMDB databases but they're fully separated and
for each agent you have exactly one Signify client and they have a secure API between them. Okay. So
client C cannot talk to agent B.
All of your state will be held in the cloud. Signify is completely stateless except for the key material
that you give to generate and derive the keys. All signing happens at the edge. So Signify will push signed
events up into KERIA and your agent will you know do whatever with that. It'll make propagate it to wit‐
nesses send an exchange message to a verifier or so forth.
67
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
And all interactions with the rest of the KERI network. So agents, witnesses, watchers, controllers
and so forth, all happen via KERIA. And these will all be direct CESR inbound and outbound streams. So
the Signify clients will never talk directly to the other agents, witnesses and watchers.
It's always through your agent directly. And if I take that diagram and maybe zoom in a little bit on
KERIA. Again we have the three clients and three agents. Each agent has its isolated database, and
KERIA itself is a set of HTTP endpoints. So there are three endpoints 3901, 3902 and 3903.
The last one 3903 is used to provision new agents. Okay. So if you come along if say Signify client D
installs a wallet on their phone which uses Signify and they go through the onboarding process, they'll be
able to provision a new agent through this endpoint which will set up all the database and so forth. And
that endpoint uses the the “agency” it's called internally, and the agency has its own database to track
you know which client relates to which agent, which identifier that was created by a client relates to
which agent.
It's pretty small, to be honest. This port 3903 in production shouldn't be left open because otherwise
you could just write a loop and keep provision new agents with more databases. So in Veridian we com‐
pletely block that port from public access and we have one-time boot URLs that are sent by emails to
protect the infrastructure.
Once the client is onboarded in and they have their agent up and running within KERIA in the ser‐
vice they can then use the 3901 port on the top left which is the admin API. This is just a set of REST
endpoints, create identifier issue ACDC and so forth.
It's completely authenticated. So of course only one client can talk to one agent. We'll see more on
that in the coming slides. And then your agent, it might be talking to other nodes in the KERI network to
say "propagate events for witness receipts", and so forth or send an exn message. Like everything in the
KERIA implementation, everything's async. So the response will often come back in on a diﬀerent port.
When it comes in, it'll come on to the message router. And the message router will based on the
headers it'll look up in the agency: "okay, someone external is trying to talk to this AID which agent
should I route to", and it'll route to the correct agent and they'll parse the incoming CESR stream using
that database specifically.
So that's kind of generally operationally how it works and again just to recap there are three diﬀerent
ports. The boot port is used to provision new agents. The admin port serves all the rest endpoints for the
clients and the external 3902 port is the CESR endpoint for inbound interactions; okay? So if we to delve
a bit deeper into the trust relationship between KERIA and Signify. So, you've onboarded into the system
but how do you actually authenticate on that API?

lem with the signed-header approach is that you have your authenticity because everything's signed by
the AIDs but you don't have your confidentiality. You have to lean on TLS. So if the TLS connection was
compromised, you lose confidentiality. Whereas with ESSR it's basically your best way of combining au‐
thenticity and confidentiality. So sign -, combining encryption and digital signatures.
It's what is used by SPAC and the trust spanning protocol. So Phil [Feairheller, red.] implemented it
in KERIPy. We've extended it down to the API between Signify and KERIA. It looks something like this. I
won't spend too long on this, but it's designed with Sam [Smith, red.]. It's like a HTTP request tunneling
kind of thing. So the green at the top left is the original request Which we would send say HTP request
for put 'slash' identifiers with the body and the headers and so forth.
We serialize it into a HTTP string and then we do a cryptobox seal with Libsodium to encrypt it to
the agent AID. So only the agent can decrypt it. Cryptobox seal is a form of hybrid public key encryption.
So for those of you who are aware, asymmetric keys are incredibly ineﬃcient at encrypting data. it's just
70
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
not really feasible.
Symmetric keys are much quicker. The crypto box seal from Libsodium is a form of hybrid public key
encryption which uses your asymmetric key to derive a symmetric key essentially. I don't know the inter‐
nals of it, but it's what SPAC uses.
This sealed box then can become the body of a new request which we send to this post 'slash' end‐
point. So when we do this, every request that's sent from Signify client, nobody knows where it came
from. They don't know what we're even trying to do. They don't know that we're trying to update an
identifier because it's going to post.
Only the agent will be able to decrypt it. And in the headers, we put a signature over this, essentially.
Now, the signature needs to be over the digest of the body, a timestamp for replay-attack protection, and
the sender and receiver AIDs.
And additionally in encrypt sender sign receiver, you also have to encrypt the sender. That's why the
sender is also a header of the original request which was encrypted. Only then are you fully secure from
all the attacks that Sam (Smith, red) mentions in his SPAC paper.
Okay. So yeah we've gone over the general architecture and also gone through the trust relationship.
Now, finally, the last kind of piece to tie it up will be how you use KERIA to interact with the rest of the
world. Up until this point, you've basically installed a wallet and onboarded onto your cloud agent and
done nothing else.
The first thing you'll probably do is create an identifier, maybe resolve a OOBI, but probably create an
identifier. In the codebase these are known as managed AIDs because the client AID we created earlier is
only used indirect mode to talk to your agent.
It's only for that communication. It's not used to receive credentials, issue credentials or anything
else. We can use the '/' identifiers endpoint on KERIA to create managed AIDs. And these are, well, they
may be private or public bur these are AIDs you would use to interact with the rest of the world or use in
your use case, they're more user facing.
But once again the keys remain at the edge. Now this is a bit technical but for those of you who know
KERIPy: In KERIPy you have a 'Hab', which represents an AID and you can incept a Hab and you can sign
with a Hab and so forth. You can have group Habs. Also within KERIPy there are Signify Habs and
Signify group Habs. It's just so the code knows that the keys are not readily available. The keys exist
somewhere else at the edge.
So if you see that in the code that's why it exists. Now, you might be asking where did the keys come
from? Maybe a naive approach would be to use the passcode that we used to create the client AID and
derive more keys. That would be problematic because of passcode rotation and it also would go against
what Sam said this morning of being able to use just your KEL to derive exactly which sequence number
on the derivation paths is required.
So actually for for each new AID you actually create a new salt. They're fully separated. It's of course
randomly generated by Signify at the edge. And it's stored encrypted on KERIA. This is the default mode
in Signify.
71
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
It's of course encrypted with the client AID. And you have a salty keeper which tracks you know, for
this AID, what encrypted salt does it have, what derivation pad is it currently on, how many rotation
events is done, and so forth.
The main reason you would want to split these is if you did a passcode rotation and your one pass‐
code had all your keys for all of your identifiers when you want to rotate that passcode for whatever rea‐
son, you would have to issue a rotation event for every single AID. And that would get very cumbersome
as well if you also had group AIDs. You had to coordinate that. So this way there's a kind of a clean sepa‐
ration kept from them.
You can also .., I think Phil had this in his last presentation as well, you can also use' Randy', which
means, just instead of a salt, which is used to be stretched with argon 2 ID to generate keys, you can just
randomly generate keys directly and store them encrypted in KERIA; same idea. this is kind of goes back
to that first slide I had where from the spec that your keypair storage can be done by your agent.
Now in case you don't want to sign with Signify.
In case you don't want to sign with Signify or expose your salt over the internet like that, you can also
use an external source for signing such as a HSM. This, I believe there's a current open PR in the commu‐
nity to fix that on KERIA. But you can use HSMs and directly sign all of your key events externally.
Now once you've created an identifier so you would generate that inception event generate the salt
whatever post that to KERIA, to communicate with the rest of the world you would need to generate an
OOBI. So each managed ID can now set up their OOBI. This is probably going to get a bit technical now
but if you know how OOBIs work this will make sense. If you don't, this is probably too much informa‐
tion, but in KERI nodes or whatever can have various roles in the system such as witness, watcher, agent,
controller.
If you were to build a wallet with just just KERIPy and not have the split of controller, agent, your

My name is Fergal. I'm the identity solutions lead architect at the Cardano Foundation. I've been

Over a year ago, we launched the Veridian wallet to the public app stores. This is the first

eﬀort into bringing a nice user experience to the user. You know abstracting away the details of low-level
KERI and giving something that's actually usable to the end user. So in doing that we had to actually con‐

Yesterday I spoke about the security architecture of KERI and Signify. But today I'll talk about the
path to production. I think there are some maturity gaps in the implementation of KERI and Signify that
should be addressed for adoption of the KERI suite. It's starting to lag behind the maturity of KERIPy.
We'll kind of step through that today.
So, I mean yesterday's talk gave kind of an in-depth explanation of how it works in practice. But kind
of the tagline that I had was agent in the cloud controller at the edge. So KERIA allows you to deploy
cloud agents which essentially handle all of the verification of KERI/ACDC/CESR up in the cloud and al‐
low you to use an edge client library known as Signify at the edge to just assign key events essentially or
ACDCs this keeps the edge layer very thin.
For example, Veridian wallet on our mobile just uses a Signify-ts to sign things essentially and all of
the complex validation logic is pushed to the cloud kind of like a black box essentially. So in many ways it
becomes a more easy to use API that's higher level which is desirable for adoption It gives something
that's a bit more tangible for people to develop on.
And very quickly, this is taken from my deck yesterday, but doesn't look so good on the four screens
there but if KERIA is this kind of block in the middle is the multi- agent service it's based on KERIPy. So
it's a Python service. It allows you to spin up various agents in the cloud that are fully isolated. So they
have separate databases. And for every agent in the cloud, you have exactly one Signify client at the edge.
So this these could be various applications from various people sharing the same cloud agent. This could
be on a mobile device for example. And there's a secure communication between each client and agent.

but it pretty much almost is. And instead of adding types directly into Signify, we've actually taken a kind
of A diﬀerent approach because as I mentioned yesterday, Signify essentially is an API wrapper around
KERIA. It can do extra things like generate key events and sign them, but it's otherwise an API wrapper.
And since you have an API on KERIA, you can let KERIA decide what it wants and what it will respond
with through its open API documentation. It will say at this endpoint, it will give a response in this for‐
mat if it's a HTTP 200 or whatever. So, and this is common practice, to take open API documentation and
autogenerate types in languages. So that's what we've done: We've updated all of the open API docs on
KERIA and generated types in Signify-ts and Signify Java because they propagate to various languages
now as a single source of truth and if you wanted to add in another Signify like a Rust library you could
just automatically generate them. Now you might have to do some reworking in places.
Java for example is not as expressive in its type system as TypeScript is. It didn't play nice with a lot of
the semantics of CESR. But we've kind of got it working now. So it took some changes there but it's quite
useful and also we're not hand rolling the open API docs. We're actually taking the data structures in
KERIPy that Sam has written saying "Here are the top level fields of an ACDC, we've converted those
into Python data classes automatically generated so they can be reused in KERIA and those data classes

more accessible. Yeah, I mean, if KERIA and Signify were to disappear, you know, everything we're doing
is built on open protocols. You don't have open protocols. You don't have any lock-in to particular soft‐
ware stacks. You know, I'm sure the community would find other ways and there already are existing oth‐

So everyone, I'm George McEwan. I'm the privacy architect for the state of Utah. So there's a lot of
synchronicity that happens when you go to these various events. And amazingly enough, I'm going
to talk a little bit about healthcare in the rural healthcare setting and why we look at it the way we
do. One of the things I'm going to make very clear is that the state is going to declare some boundaries in
what it does in the KERI infrastructure and what the expectations are for the community because one of
the things we learned as I was the IT director during the COVID era at the department of health, there
was definitely some resistance to where government had oﬀered the technologies or applications or
worked with platform providers to provide things like exposure notification and the backlash was very in‐
tense. That's probably the best word I can use. And so we realize that where government can help and we
can set regulations and we can set frameworks in play, there's definitely a spot where the government has
an edge and we say this is private sector and for the individual to handle.
So, what I'm going to do is I'm going to run you through a little bit of a presentation that we do with
we'll call them non-nerds, normal people. Because we can talk in shorthand in the SEDI community and
in the KERI community and cyber security. We have words they don't understand. So, what we did is we
put together kind of a generic presentation that we add slides to depending on who we're talking to.
So, realistically, we hit them on these points. Digital identity is here. It's coming. It's going to be
something. Now, one of the, I would say, the beauties of the SEDI program is Chris Bramwell said, "Well,
we could take this and put this against what are the constitutional rights that someone has."
And by doing so, you get past all of the vendor needs to make money. You get you get right down to
the root of the problem, which is there needs to be accountability and that your rights need to be pre‐
served and that privacy is not impossible when you are talking about digitally issued credentials. And of
course then we get into the stakes.
If the government doesn't choose or regulate, the market does it itself. And the goals of a open mar‐
ket are a little bit diﬀerent than what a government needs to be providing the services that a citizen
would want. And of course, we know that besides governments, there's also organizations that put to‐
gether specifications that may or may not be rooted in the same value system that we're trying to main‐
tain. So we're very adamant that we want to make sure that whatever we do in the SEDI environment, it's
reflective of the values, goals, and rights that are held here in America. Now, it is not hard for this state‐
ment to be believed by all of you, right? Whoever has the data has the control. And the model right now,
everything to the left or sorry, my right everything over there is how we work today. Your identity is
owned by other individuals. It's granted back to you with limited rights. And then if you've ever looked
through all those long user licenses, in eﬀect, you've given up all control for the services that you have.
And this isn't limited to just, hey, I got myself an email address for free. Your financial institutions do
this, your hospitals and your HMOs, your care organizations, they're holding a lot of the data that be‐
longs to you. And because it's set up that way, you are basically powerless to do anything about it. And in
a lot of cases, you'll find that incorrect data may be used in profiling you to create an improper picture of
who you are.
So really what we want to accomplish inside the SEDI framework is the idea that you are the individ‐
ual who controls that information because wherever the information sits, it is fair to say without contra‐
diction that impacts the financial outcomes. It impacts your opportunities. There are a lot of things that
get impacted because you don't control what should rightfully be yours. And this is not to say that every‐
one did it for a nefarious purpose. I mean, profit-seeking motive.
103
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
We're a capitalist society. Yeah, that's understood. But the problem is we built everything to be so ex‐
ploitative of you that we forgot that you are the individual who ultimately gets hurt if something goes
wrong. In the state of Utah, inside government, there's a very good way that we look at data. When we
make a data error in government, it's a negative outcome for a real person. Classic example, oh the
Medicaid eligibility system, which is a real time system, goes down for a couple hours. Now, if you're

industry has real world implications. So the why for us, why we're here, why we're doing this is because
as we increasingly move care into a digital context, we increasingly make the risk to patients explode.
And why is that? Well, if you shut down a hospital using a ransomware attack, the first response that
hospital has to do from a care perspective is send every ambulance that's supposed to come to them
someplace else. And especially in the US that ecosystem is incredibly fragile. And so if you're talking
about for example an urban center, they may have to push hospitals out to hospitals in rural centers and
those hospitals may not have the same equipment, they may not have the same training, they may not
have a level one trauma center. So if you shut down a large health system, you are actively threatening
the lives of patients. In fact, NPR about two years ago put out a report that stated that a ransomware at‐
tack on healthcare should be considered as disastrous as a natural disaster. So a tornado or hurricane,

KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
they are the same level of disruption. The diﬀerence of course is while we can blame God for tornadoes
and hurricanes we blame Russia and North Korea for the ransomware attacks that are happening in the
industry. So a little bit of data on this. Healthcare is the most targeted industry by a large margin when
you look at some of these other pieces. There's a few reasons for this. They're also the most expensive.
So, this is part of what's driving that. You'll get the biggest payout if you're holding a knife to the throat
of an organization that has to pay you or people will die. And so, attackers know this. This is a persistent
data source. This isn't just one year. This has been for the last decade. Healthcare has been the most tar‐
geted industry. So one of the questions we get all the time is why attack patient data? What's the value
proposition for an attacker to come into this ecosystem and to compromise all of this data? The reality is
there's a few diﬀerent things. The first is that on the black market, a patient record is typically 10x or
more the value of let's say a credit card, which makes perfect sense, right? You your credit card gets com‐
promised. What's the first thing you do? You call up Capital One or Chase and you say, "Hey, I lost my
card." And within a week, they've got a new card for you, right? They've rotated you to a new credit card
number. And you're back up and running. When you lose your medical record, you're losing your social
security number, you're losing your date of birth, you're losing your name, you're losing typically one or
two payment methods, and you're also losing all of your medical information itself. None of that can be
rotated. As much as I'd love to call Capital One and say, "Hey, you know, I've got brain cancer. That's
great. Can we rotate this to like diabetes?" I'd prefer that it just doesn't work that way. You have im‐
mutable data in a health record, which means that the value of that data lasts for as long as the data is
valid, which is forever. So with that data, there are a number of diﬀerent things that they can do. So one
is just selling the records online but beyond that it's things like standing up fake clinics to execute fraud
to CMS and it I can prove to CMS that I'm treating you and I can do that at a scale of hundreds or thou‐
sands of clinics.
There was actually a clinic, this was two years ago, I say “clinic”, there was a group in Florida that
was found to have absolutely no patients, but they had a whole lot of data and they were billing CMS
pretty aggressively to get money out of that system. Other things to note here is and this one I actually
want to hit pretty heavily which is extortion attacks. It used to be that the primary attack surface that an
attacker was looking for, the monetization that they wanted was if I hit a hospital, they'll pay me $7 mil‐
lion to unlock my data. And that's still true.
But these days, the attacker actually also couples that with exfiltration of that data. And because AI
has made the cost per extortion so low, they'll now go after individual patients as well. So they'll go after
.., a recent article I read was that a bunch of mimography images were captured and they were actually
going to individual women and saying, "Hey, we have compromising images of you from this this data
that we pulled out of your health system. You're going to pay us $2,000 or we're going to put these out
on the dark web." And so it used to be that $2,000 wasn't enough to get anybody interested. These days,
because it is so simple and easy to make that attack, and by the way, I already have all your contact infor‐
mation, we can just set up a script and the AI can extort for us.
Which is obviously not terrible but when we ask about 'Why is healthcare so compromised?', it's be‐
cause it's insecure it's high value and the data doesn't change across time which makes it a really good
find and can be resold multiple times in the future. The other problem here is, not only is healthcare's
data incredibly valuable, it is also incredibly complex. So you have health systems, you have health pay‐
ers. These are typically the two big players in the space. Each of these ecosystems will on average have
about 1,500 connections. So the average health system has got vendors that are connecting into their sys‐

tem doing a number of diﬀerent tasks for them. Each one of these connections represents an attack sur‐

All of the credit card information in Target got stolen. The way that they got in was through the

Eventually, you're looking for a sysadmin role.
Once you have a sysadmin role, you have keys of the kingdom. You execute your ransomware. You

get back up and running. So that's the attack in a nutshell and this is the risk. So again thousands of con‐
nections coming into the system each one of them representing a potential attack surface for bad actors
to enter in. So we used to think and I think some people still do that if we've encrypted the channel then
we have a secure connection and the reality is that the attacker will most often use that channel against
you hunting for what we call shared secrets. This is a shared credential. This is username and password.
This is a bearer token.
There's a number of diﬀerent things that qualify as a shared secret. Our contention is that we proba‐
bly should stop letting them steal these shared secrets. Impersonating your third party, moving through
the channel that you trusted to be secure, and then exfiltrating your data. It's time for us to actually re‐
duce the attack surface by taking this piece here and removing the shared secrets that come into the sys‐
tem so that those attacks are no longer valid, no longer reasonable for an attacker to execute. Now, we've
talked about earlier today a lot of the value in terms of the connectivity problem with regards to onboard‐
ing, with regards to the manual lift on it. One of the other things that KERI does for us is this. It allows
us to create an ecosystem of true zero trust where there is no compromising credential for participants to
be attacked on.

then they come together and start to put them into mandates. CMS- 0057 basically says that by January
1, 2027, every payer that receives Medicare and Medicaid dollars has to stand up an API for these four
use cases, which is payer-to-payer exchange, patient access, payer to provider exchange, and then a

My name is Jared Jeﬀrey. I'm CEO and founder at HealthKERI. Really excited to be here at the KERI
conference today. We just did a session on using the VLEI in healthcare to basically solve the
problem of organizational identity. So with healthcare organizations, there's really not a good
identifier to help you understand which organization you're talking to. So as just a quick example, if you
talk about St. Mary's, well, which St. Mary's? There's, you know, 17 of them. And which St. Mary's owned
by which organization? Because several St. Mary's are owned by the same organization. Several are
owned, you know, by other organizations. So really what we're trying to solve here is can we bootstrap
rapid connectivity using KERI and the VLEI? Yeah, so I think one of the things that I see maybe diﬀerent‐
ly than the rest of the world here is that everybody else has kind of adopted now 25-year-old security
technology, you know, tokens, OAuth, OAuth, things of that nature that were really never designed for an
ecosystem that exists here today. So we've kind of carried this legacy architecture into the modern world
where not only do you have way more organizations doing connections, you know, real time, but you also
have things like not just AI internally being used in LLMs, but agentic AI. So you have kind of created an
entirely new entity class for connectivity. And I think a lot of people are still trying to take this round
hole and fill it with the old square peg of last year's cybersecurity protocols. So what would the decentral‐
ized identity system lose if all of our work at HealthKERI had evaporated? Well, I think the easiest an‐
swer is as of today, we put a ton of code into the open source. So one of the biggest complaints we heard
about people that didn't want to adopt KERI or were slow to move into a KERI-based ecosystem was that
KERI was too complicated and the infrastructure wasn't there. We recognize that a rising tide raises all
boats. And so we just recently put into open source a production ready wallet that can handle KERI
identifiers.
We put into open source witnesses and watcher code as well. Those three components are three of
the five Ws that are being asked for from the KERI that are being asked for from the KERI Foundation.
So by putting those into open source, we're really putting a commitment into this ecosystem saying we
want all of the parties that are interested in seeing KERI be successful come to the table and collaborate
with us. So if all of that went away today, you would lose probably two years worth of solid work from
one of the best developers, Phil Feairheller on bringing healthKERI or KERI at large into existence.

This version of the Locksmith app is an open-source stripped down version of what healthKERI has.
So, if you guys want to follow along, I have a open source repo. It's
https://github.com/jaelliot/locksmith-demo and you can follow along. There's some instructions in
the readme.
Locksmith is the desktop wallet for KERI. This talk is about the controller side of the system where
the keys stay local. Users manage their identifiers and control does not disappear into a single provider
stack. users need a wallet surface where the keys in control.
The trust root lives at the edge and non-hosted services. In KERI the controller is the part that actual‐
ly controls the AID. That means that it manages the keys in the key event life cycle. If that user-facing
wallet gets absorbed into one vertically integrated service stack, the system may still look open on paper,
but it becomes less open in practice. That's why the wallet boundary matters is where the control actually
lives.
Many services describe themselves as open, but in practice, the wallet, the provider, and the integra‐
tion path are all tied to the same stack. When that happens, builder choice starts to disappear for KERI
to remain genuinely open. The wallet must remain separate, and that separation must be real and verifi‐
able. So, with that in mind, here's where Locksmith sits in the stack.
Locksmith sets the controller side wallet layer. Witnesses stay outside of it. The watchers stay outside
of it. The connectors… the providers connect to it but they do not become the wallet. And by "providers"
I mean the real external services, witness and watcher operators, credential services, workflow services,
and other KERI-adjacent integrations.
The first thing that Locksmith proves is the local state. It is a real local identity vault. It supports
multiple vaults and local first storage and controller-owned state on disk. That matters because portabili‐
ty could come later. The first proof is local control.
– What are you defining as portability here? You could deploy it to any location, I suppose? – Okay.
Because you say state ownership for portability. I imagine that means the key state and everything neces‐
sary for like interacting with the service provider.
Is that what you mean by state ownership? Yeah. The identifier credentials and real user workloads is
where the wallet becomes real for the user. Locksmith is the desktop surface where someone can create
and manage identifiers, track credential state, work with KERI operations through a usable interface.
That is what the wallet layers for turning core KERI behavior into something a person could actually use.
This is where the architecture stays open. Locksmith has a real plug-in boundary and code, providers
sit behind that boundary rather than replace it. That means that the wallet can integrate with outside ser‐
vices without collapsing into infrastructure. What is real today is the boundary itself. What is in progress
is the broader ecosystem around it. More provider side integrations and the KERI Foundation's work to
carry that pattern through more completely. So the architecture for now is real, and the full provider sto‐
ry is currently being built.
This is our architecture flow. The user signs and stores locally in the Locksmith vault. From there, the
wallet can communicate with provider side services via the plug-in boundary. Separately, events go out to
witnesses and watchers and receipts come back.

So, decentralized identifiers or DIDs. It is a W3C oﬃcial recommendation As of several years ago.
There's a 1.0 core spec and I think they're working on a 2.0 spec now. You can click through to the actual
specification there at on that hyperlink.
It's a type of identifier. It's formally a URI identifier that allows an entity to communicate things like
cryptographic material keys, Verification methods, and or service endpoints like say an authorized agent.
And this communication happens by resolving is the term the DID. So, you resolve the DID like you
would resolve a URL. And when you resolve a URL, you get back a usually a chunk of HTML like a web‐
page or something like that, some resource. When you resolve a DID, what you get back is a DID docu‐
ment and the DID document will contain information like what are your current public keys, do you have
any authorized agents, etc. etc.
So, the scheme for a DID is you have the mandatory URI scheme prefix, which is DID, the colon,
similar to a URL. So, like in a URL if it's the HTTP, you know, and then whatever. This is in this case it's
DID, whatever. The DID method name, so in the case of DID:Webs this would be Webs. And then each
DID method has its own method specific identifier that's determined by the designers of the specification
for that DID method. This is an example of an imaginary one.
You have, in addition to the DID document, the DID method itself describes how do you create, up‐
date, delete, you know, etc. the DID documents, and every DID method has its own mechanism for that,
along with its own method specific identifier.
There are currently hundreds of DID methods out there in various states of standardization. Many are
associated with specific blockchain technologies. There are lots of other creative ones. There are a few
that deal specifically with web and web protocols and web infrastructure, of which DID:Webs is one.

So, because of DID:Web having the shortcomings that it has, a number of designers have proposed

But what DID:Webs does is it separates discoverability from verifiability. So we use the web to discov‐
er the information about the identifier, and you can use the DID document to get the keys and all of that.
But, if you want to verify the information in the DID document, you do that via KERI events through
something we call the KERI Event Stream, the KERI Event Stream, which is kind of an aggregation of
various KERI events from various sources that may be relevant to the controller, and that's aggregated
into this KERI event stream. So, to resolve a DID:Webs, you actually are resolving two resources, two
endpoints. You resolve the did.json and get the DID document, but then you also resolve the KERI
CESR, and you have to validate that just like you would any other KERI Event Stream. We have a ques‐

Jonathan-Rayback-Interview
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · INTERVIEWS · 01:26
145
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
H i, I'm Jonathan Reibach. My presentation was on DIDWebs and the connection between KERI and
the decentralized identifier ecosystem. Well, I think KERI oﬀers some unique qualities that really
help DIDWebs be the strongest version of the DIDWeb family of identifiers. Specifically, it allows
for the controller to explicitly authorize information like host information, path information. And it also
allows for much more convenience when dealing with very, very long-lived identifiers like over the span
of decades, a lifetime. I think those two advantages are really strong for DIDWebs. I think if the work
that we're doing on DIDWebs disappeared tomorrow, it would be too bad. I think that there as long as
there's a decentralized identified identifier market and infrastructure out there, having a bridge between
that ecosystem and the KERI ecosystem makes a lot of sense. There are going to be consumers of KERI
information that want to get that via a DID document because their infrastructure is set up that way. And
I think if we lost DIDWebs, KERI would lose a foothold in the interoperability space. That would be too
bad.!

KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Most people use it as either a browser app or like on a cross-platform application basis, Ionic

I'll preface this by saying there's two broad categorizations of wallets in the KERI ecosystem. The ter‐
minology that I'm about to use is mine. I started using this because it's it's a way for me to categorize
how the diﬀerent wallets are.
You can use whatever terminology makes the most sense to you. I call the Signify client wallet,
Signify and KERIA architecture, I say lightweight wallet. And I call what Locksmith is, Philip Feairheller
from healthKERI, Locksmith is a heavyweight wallet. Why is that the case? I'm about to describe why
and we're just you're going to understand it from the very beginning of the presentation.
Lightweight refers to what the user has in their hands. With Signify, all they have is an edge signing
client. They don't store their credentials. Keys only ever exist unencrypted in memory in whatever brows‐
er or web view instance they have, but that's all that's ever stored here is just the unencrypted keys and
just enough state to do whatever your operation you're doing, whether it's issuing credentials, doing an
interaction event, doing a key rotation, doing an OOBI resolution. A bare minimum of state is kept here,
which is why I call it the lightweight client.
The heavyweight client essentially takes, you could say this edge signing architecture, and your key
store, heavyweight wallets put all that together locally. You could think of local first software where all of
your keys .., if you'd have a heavy weight wallet, you'd have everything in in one app on your phone, and
you would not have a server agent. The lightweight wallet has a server agent, and you might say, "Well, is
that okay from a security perspective? What about the keys? Where are the keys? Are we custodying keys
here?"
We're going to get into the nuts and bolts in the later slides. The answer is that there are keys, key
seeds that are stored here, but they're only ever stored in the server encrypted. The way that it works,
and we'll get… there's some more slides… is the root master key here in the Signify lightweight wallet
client is an encryption decryption key.
It's also a KERI AID. It performs a delegation from the in-memory identifier to the agent identifier. If
you're familiar with the concept of a witness, a witness is a non-transferable identifier, right? And but it's
a regular KERI identifier. You can get a key event log. You can resolve an out-of-band identifier to get that
Key Event Log with that one event plus some location scheme records and endpoint rule records for a
witness. Well, an agent is a little bit like a witness, and there's a common capability between witnesses
and agents at least in the most commonly deployed form. HealthKERI actually did witnesses the right
way in the sense that it's only a witness.
But, just because of an artifact of accident of history, witnesses in the beginning of GLEIF's imple‐
mentation of the the KERI spec, witnesses and mailboxes were combined. Mailbox is a store and forward
mechanism. Cuz what happens if your phone goes oﬄine and it's a lightweight client? You still want to
be able to receive messages when that phone comes back online, right? You don't want to not be able to
receive messages because your phone's oﬀ or it's dead battery.
156
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So, what you do is there's a mailbox. A mailbox runs in a persistent process that has an IP address, a
way to essentially send it messages, which is similar to a DIDCOM relay. Now, I see that a couple of peo‐
ple came in here later. Before I sort of finish oﬀ the mailbox topic, I'm just giving you a warning. I'm go‐
ing to come in to ask your names and sort of introduce you to everyone else cuz we're all developers here,
so we get to to know each other. All right, so I'm talking about this concept of a mailbox.
An agent functions as a mailbox. It's a store and forward mechanism. It's a persistent internet pres‐
ence. The primary responsibilities of an agent, it's a mailbox, it's a delegation communication proxy,
which we'll get into later, and it's the primary data store.
Your ACDCs are stored here, not here. Your contacts, your out-of-band identifiers, your Key Event
Log, it's all stored here. It's all signed here. Nothing is signed here on your identifiers. With a small
caveat, remember I said it's a mailbox, it's a store and forward mechanism, it's a delegation communica‐
tion proxy.
As it performs those functions, remember, we're in zero trust. It's a signed everything architecture, no
shared secrets architecture. When the agent itself, which is an identifier, it's an actor in this ecosystem,
when the agent is sending communications, it does have its local key that it'll sign messages so that
everything coming from the agent can be signed and verified.
But, the agent does not have any of your keys. That's the thing. Go ahead, question. – Is this strictly a
delegation model? – Strictly delegation. KERI delegation. Okay. And what's interesting, so how it works
and this is… architecture slides are fun because you can spend a lot of time on them and then we'll
breeze through the rest of the the slides.

thread .., it's like a fundamental construct where it doesn't matter if you block within one thread and the
reason why you use threads is because you want to have a main thread that's not blocked by work that
will block that you put to another thread.
If you want to adopt the mindset of structured concurrency, you need to think from a non-blocking
mindset. Often it's called a reactive mindset. So zero of the co-routines should block. And you might
wonder, how is that possible?

blocking way. And how do you do that? You essentially chop up your IO reads and writes into chunks

The biggest benefit is my first bullet point here. Program flow or task flow follows lexical scope. Now,
what does that mean? It means that the execution of the program will follow your intuition as a pro‐
grammer. You're going to write a function. You expect control flow to go from the beginning of the func‐
tion to the end.
And when that function is over, you expect operations you've created in that function to have com‐
pleted. Unless you're doing threads or processes, in which case you might return a handle from the func‐
tion to sort of go later, but structured concurrency, usually the mental model is if I have a function that
fires oﬀ a bunch of tasks, all of those should complete by the time the function returns. Okay. And so
what you're doing is you're turning the function into a bit of a almost like a scheduler of a bunch of sub‐
tasks. Are you familiar with Erlang and a process supervisor?
No. Okay. That's a good mental model. Erlang or Elixir? No. so it's there's a process super supervision
model. But I'll leave that terminology aside. It'll be clearer as I get further into the code examples, but es‐
sentially the idea is that if I enter into a function and that function has tasks in it, there's a guarantee that
structured concurrency provides that all those tasks will finish or be canceled by the time that I leave that
function.

Okay, so other important guarantees of structured concurrency, if you spawn in a lexical scope, you
will join before that lexical scope returns. And in HIO that means if you have a DoDoer and you spin up
some child processes, those will get to the done state before the DoDoer finishes. So, the Doist will own
all of its children will complete before the Doist returns.
And some nice consequences of this is then we have straightforward execution handling. Failure can‐
cellation propagation throughout the task tree is very intuitive from child tasks up to parent tasks. And
now you can intelligently respond to both failures and cancellation signals.

I' m Kent Bull and my session was KERIA tutorial talking about how to set up a KERIA server, what a

with the signature? If Signify TS and KERIA disappeared, what the self-sovereign identity community

an agent and a lightweight wallet implementation. It's very useful for certain, I would say constrained
contexts useful technology, we'd have to re -engineer it, but that's what we'd lose if Signify and KERIA

Welcome to the session on Secure Asset Transfer Protocol. I'm Ned Smith. I happen to be Sam's
brother. So I've been introducing myself as Sam's brother for a long time. I'm the younger one.
But I have a 30-year career at Intel and then another eight years in industry. All of it related to
security. So Sam's the newcomer in the group to security, just so you know. And I've also been a prolific
inventor. So I have over 500 granted patents and I've been interested in doing some work in the IETF.
Most recently I have been looking at the SATP protocol in one of the working groups in the IETF. So I'm
going to just give you a quick overview of what the IETF is as a standards organization and how the
SATP group fits in there. So IETF is the acronym for internet engineering task force. It's really a standard
setting body that's recognized internationally.
It is the body that defined the internet protocol. So it is all things internet originated in the IETF. The
organization itself is structured around diﬀerent areas. So there's a internet area, a routing area, security
operations, general applications and real time. There's a few others that I didn't mention here.
And within the applications in real time area, also called ART, there's a working group called Secure
Asset Transfer. And they're the ones that are doing the work to define a secure asset transfer protocol.
And I'm going to talk about that the rest of this talk.
To dive in here, there's a what you might think of as the canonical use case in SATP to drive the tech‐
nical portions of it. So I'm going to walk through this use case. It's essentially a token issuance use case.
And this token use case, it's built around this something called the asset reference token or art not to be
confused with the working group. But it's a sort of a reference token for for working through the chal‐
lenges associated with building systems around tokenized assets and stable coins. In this use case the to‐
ken issuer in step one issues and declares an asset that is then given to an artifacts registry to make it
persist there so so that the asset is known to exist. In step two, the issuer mints a token. And we're using
the acronym network one or NW1 to refer to the token issuer side of this exchange.
And they mint the token and in this token, there's sort of the value part or part one, which is the part
that is on the chain. And then there's something called context data, or part two, which refers to any arti‐
facts that are not on-chain or maybe partially on-chain but in some way represent some stability to the
stable coin asset.
The importance of that linking will come in a little bit later in the context of the asset network. So in
this case, they've minted the minted the coin and then there's an entity that is the interface to the asset
management network. So we're calling this, network one, asset network one. And that's where you inter‐
face to whatever is your distributed ledger technology, whether it's a public, private, or even a monolithic
Network.
And then in step four, the gateway reads a token from network one, which we're calling the SATP
gateway one, and that then in step five interfaces with the artifacts registry to collect the additional data
that's there and it validates that prior to the asset transfer. And then in step six, this is sort of the jump‐
ing oﬀ point for actually doing the transfer over the SATP protocol. And the idea is you're transferring an
asset from gateway one or the token issuance domain into another network which is receiving that asset.
What are the SATP entities? There's something called the originator which is the entity that owns the
asset and then the beneficiary which is the entity that's receiving the asset. There's a set of hosts that are
in the model for how that exchange happens.
181
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So there's the gateway which are the endpoints of the protocol. There's an asset network or an oracle
that is the controller of all the domain assets. Anything that's relevant to the context in which that gate‐
way operates is represented by this notion of an asset network; could be one or many hosts.
Then there's the user application or orchestrator which is the kind of the controller or the entity
that's driving the you know the asset transfer that's coordinating the resources and and giving the in‐
structions to the gateways to present their assets on the protocol. There's another entity which I call the
host owner. It's not in the first iterations of the protocol. It's not really called out directly, but it's implied
in the some of the some of the message exchanges that appear in stage one of the protocol. So I'm calling
it out as it's a real entity and we'll call it the host owner for now just to make the slides more clear, but
it's these entities that are responsible for administering the infrastructure and it represents the entities
that are responsible for that are legally responsible for the asset.
There's also something called an artifacts registry. Think of it as a directory that maintains asset
transfer context and makes it u persistent for whatever additional Your investigation that's needed on the
asset. It's something that provides the audit trail for the protocol.
So here is a view of the architecture for SATP. One of the main goals of this architecture is to essen‐
tially implement a an atomic two-phase commit protocol for the asset exchange. It's fundamentally de‐
centralized. In other words, there's no assumption that the originator and the beneficiary share a trusted
third party relationship.
There's a tokenized and non-tokenized asset. So, not everything is on-chain per se. But it can exist
and it is relevant to the to the exchange of assets. It needs to be auditable and and the assets need to be
durable. There's also the notion of a canonical identifier, which is to say that the representation of the
identifiers and the information that are known to the originator don't have to be the same as the repre‐
sentation of identifiers that are known to beneficiary B. In other words, the attributes that are in com‐
mon have to be negotiated or created as part of the protocol.
But it's not requiring that the asset identifier has to be morphed into a namespace that's defined by
the protocol or the artifacts registry. There's a need for asset versioning, pinning and roll back. That's part
of the two-phase commit requirement. And There's also requirements for provenenced asset transfers. In
other words, you should be able to unwind this asset transfer and be able to identify all the stages in the
transfer after the transfer has happened.
And so as you can see in the diagram, the protocol is divided into four stages. Stage zero, stage one,
two and three. And and you see the artifacts registry kind of in the middle. It's not really a separate enti‐
ty. and I'll talk more about why that is in a second.
One thing to note is that the authority model from the perspective of the architecture in the first se‐
ries of drafts, there is an assumption, that's implicit, that it's X.509 certificate based and so this is a bias
that finds its way into the specifications and there's no normative text that says it has to be X.509 but the
way that the people are thinking, their bias towards X.509 thinking, and so that tends to be an uphill bat‐
tle as you contemplate integrating your KERI concepts into this. And so part of the initial investigation
that I did into this environment, was to look at what would it take to integrate vLEI authorization into a
SATP architecture.

H i, I'm Ned Smith and I am a researcher in trusted computing. I've spent 30 years doing hardware
trusted computing and I'm also interested in secure asset transfers based on protocols that are be‐
ing defined in the Internet Engineering Task Force.
And my presentation today was to overview where we're at with the definition of the SATP protocol.
In particular, how a SATP protocol is tied to authorization mechanisms that are important for legal re‐
sponsibility when assets are transferred. Yeah, so one of the things that sets me apart as a researcher in
trusted computing is my experience working with trust both from a hardware side but also from an in‐
frastructure side. And focused a lot of my career around this notion of device attestation which is provid‐
ing evidence about the environment in which applications or workloads run so that a remote entity can
verify that the environment is trustworthy not only that the workload is trustworthy. And so I'm incorpo‐
rating that kind of thinking into to not only the work that's being done in the ITF in the RATS working
group which I chair but also in other standards and architectures that I influence.

Authenticity and Confidentiality with KERI. I like making acronyms, too.
And what that was, is a API gateway that wrapped every transaction in an ESSR packet. So, signed
and encrypted to a specific destination and provided full security between those two endpoints. No need
for TLS, no need for VPNs, anything else. And it also created a cryptographically verifiable audit log of
every single transaction.

Infrastructure for Security
Phil Feairheller
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · TALKS · 36:59
212
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Hi everyone. Thanks for showing up today. My name, for those of you who don't know me, is Phil
Feairheller and the CTO and co-founder of healthKERI. We are specializing in bringing KERI tech‐
nology, as the name would suggest, into healthcare, in particular data exchange in healthcare to
help facilitate trust across the healthcare ecosystem. I realized I forgot a intro slide, so I'm just doing it
without a slide.
So, in my job, I end up teaching KERI very often to a lot of diﬀerent people, in particular people who
have never even heard of it before. Sometimes maybe they've heard of PKI, sometimes they've heard of
X.509s, but they've never heard of KERI. And this is one of my favorite slides to teach KERI because it
starts at the very bottom of the layers that make KERI the security protocol that it is. And when Sam first
asked me to do this talk on KERI infrastructure, I immediately thought of this slide because the in‐
frastructure that I'm going to talk about is very clearly spelled out in the layers of security that make up
the KERI stack. So, I won't do the whole spiel that I usually do for this, but very quickly, starting at the
bottom of the self-certifying identifiers and CESR, for folks that who know PKI, I always like to say that
KERI solves the two hard problems of PKI. One is creating a cryptographic binding between the identifier
and the first set of keys, and the second hard problem it solves is key rotation, creating the cryptographic
binding to every other set of keys. And we are able to do that with CESR, which is our encoding format
for exchanging cryptographic primitives at scale.
So, the next layer up is pre-rotation Key Event Logs. Already touched on what pre-rotation is, and the
Key Event Logs are of course the data structure, append-only data structure that allows us to communi‐
cate changes in our key state that anyone else in the world can verify without ever having to phone home.
Layer three delegation distributed group multisig, that is something that we only talk to I only talk to
when I'm dealing with more advanced audiences, talking about KERI's ability to do delegation both at the
identifier level as well as attenuated delegation with ACDC credentials and distributed group multisig is
one of the more fun technologies in KERI. I love working with it. I love building it when we were at
GLEIF, but we don't really dig too much into it. And so then if you think about what I'm going to talk
about today, the wallets and then the watchers and the witnesses, the first three layers, one through
three that I just talked about are all in the wallet. So I'll be able to demo most of these, actually all of
these capabilities today with the wallet. It's our wallet called Locksmith. And then when you get to layer
four, witnesses and watchers, those are the other components that I'll talk about today that we've donat‐
ed it to the KERI Foundation for open source.
And those are of course the layer that allows us to make our Key Event Logs available to other people
and keep an eye on anyone else we're doing business with, keep an eye on their key state. So the
Locksmith wallet is an application that we've built over time.
It actually started as a pet project of mine because I was tired of using the command line. Most of the
work that we did at GLEIF was done building out the command line and then actually creating the entire
GLEIF-authorized representative system with the KERI command line. And at one point I just got tired
of using the command line over and over again particularly when trying to demo KERI to people. So I
just started playing around with what it would look like to create a wallet and took the KERI command
line from KERIpy and just started building little features one at a time into this and eventually it became
a tool that I was using all the time and we figured it would be very useful and started making it a produc‐
tion quality wallet.

Digital Identity Needs a Legal

Okay, I'm going to start since we're over time already. I'm glad to be here today. Title of my talk, as
you can see, is “Digital Identity Needs a Legal Foundation”. So if you were at SEDI summit with
the first two days of the week, this is related to the things I said there and just kind of goes into a
little bit more detail on how the legal foundation actually helps us out. So we spent a lot of time talking
about the cryptography and key management and all of those things.
But I think the hardest part of digital identity is actually not the technical things but legal and institu‐
tional. I spent a lot of time talking about governance and sometimes it's can be kind of boring but ulti‐
mately I think it's what gives us the foundation for making all of this work. So, think about where we're
at. We've got decentralized key management, authentic chained credentials, Serialization formats, some
implementations, open- source code.
So, the technical foundations are largely there. That doesn't mean they're done. Doesn't mean they
can't be improved, but we've made real progress. We also have things that we're still working on like tool‐
ing and ecosystem and developer experience and all of those things that make it work.
But we need to get adoption. And in order to get adoption, we have to get rid of some of the funda‐
mental blockers. Now, I'm going to talk about a couple of them today. Not necessarily all of them. I mean
because there's no end to blockers when you're trying to do something this big. But there's a couple of
them that I think we can help solve. So Sankarshan How to say his name Mukhopadhyay I think is how
you say his last name. I haven't heard it said so I'll say it that way.
Recently wrote a book about what he called the a book a paper what he called the proof gap. And for
him, the proof https://thetrustgraph.substack.com/p/the-proof-gap gap was the idea that we still rely
largely on centralized institutions to be able to prove things about ourselves.
So, our education, employment, whatever licenses we have benefits we're entitled to, all of those
things. When proof is needed, we can't generally present it directly, especially digitally in a way that
somebody else can easily verify. So even worse, I mean, if we lose that proof, we have to essentially go
back to those institutions one at a time and confirm something about ourselves, right? So that could be a
real problem.
Back 50 years ago when everything was based on paper and kept in filing cabinets, that might have
made sense because it was really all you could do. But I think everybody in this room acknowledges that
we can do better than that in a digital world and we should do better than that, not just take that old pa‐
per filing cabinet model and move it forward.
So sometimes people think of the proof gap as a technical problem and it certainly is that. There are
technical problems with moving this centralized filing cabinet model into the digital world and trying to
decentralize it and create something that's better.
But I think we can ask ourselves about the problem of governance. There are lots of ways that we
have worked out how to transfer trust in the physical world, right? One of those is very familiar to every‐
body and that is credit cards.
60 years ago, we didn't have this problem because we didn't have credit cards really at all, right?
There were bank cards and maybe had a card from your department store, right? I remember people had
Sears cards that you could use at Sears and nowhere else, right? So they were all centralized, single do‐
main and Visa first and then others followed came up with this idea that we could have a coalition of
225
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
banks put together ways that they can work together. So this involved technology, how are we going to
technically transfer information? It involved rules and processes. It involved legal agreements. Involved
all of this. And collectively we call that thing a trust framework, right? All of those technical and legal
and regulatory rules that make all of that work. But even today if you're a merchant, you've got multiple
ones of these that you have to interact with. Now, it turns out somebody saw that problem and there are
all kinds of companies. There's actually between this guy and those guys, there's like four layers of com‐
panies that make this all work. In order to make it easy for him and for you when you go in, right? He
doesn't have to have six diﬀerent terminals and all of that because somebody's solved that problem. But
still, it's a problem.
Now, imagine what happens when we've got not five diﬀerent banking system or credit card systems.
We have thousands of diﬀerent credentials. Now we're trying to navigate the sea of islands where each of
these is a separate trust framework with their own rules, their own governance. Maybe they share some
technical underpinnings at least we could hope for that. But it's still a problem. If every category of cre‐
dentials requires its own private trust framework and every verifier has to somehow be inside each of
those trust frameworks, it's a nightmare, right? Instead of four layers between the merchant and the
credit card companies, we'll have a hundred layers between you and all of the diﬀerent things that you
might need to verify in your life. Now, private governance can solve some of these problems, right? And
we've seen lots of eﬀorts over the years. IRA is one of the most recent that tries to create a coalition of
related or like-minded maybe organizations who can come together and create a trust framework that is
interoperable and I think they all kind of have this vision of we'll just get big enough that we'll solve this
problem. It won't be a problem because we'll just get big enough that everybody will be part of us but
Visa is not part of Mastercard and they never will be.
And that's always going to be a problem. So a diﬀerent way to solve this problem is to have some‐
thing underneath all and connect them. Okay, but that's just one problem. There's another problem. I
talked about this in my talk on Monday and that is an economic problem.
So in the mid 1990s, Netscape had essentially solved the digital identity problem for at least the web.
I mean, they had pretty good ideas, right? We'll have servers that will have certificates and we'll put cer‐
tificates in browsers and your browser certificate will identify you. We'll proof your identity with these
certificates. I mean, in this day and age where you can essentially get server certificates for free, you may
not remember, but back in the day, certificates were really expensive because they were all proofed.
Right now, they basically just link a domain name to a key. That's all they do. But if you want a real
proofed certificate, they cost money. Not only because it costs money to proof you, but because the com‐
pany is putting themselves out there and saying “this information is correct”. So, they're liable. So, they
cost money.
Turns out, people were not willing to pay money to shop and to bank and all of those things. They
wanted it all to be free. So we standardized on "Servers will have certificates and people will have pass‐
words" and just kind of waved our hands and hoped the problem would go away because there was mon‐
ey to be made.
So here's the two problems. How we scale trust. How do we create trust frameworks that are more
universal? And second, who's going to pay for this. Who's proofing the person using what authority,
what's the legal standing, all of those questions.
226
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
This is where I think SEDI can help us with both of these problems and I'll talk about them in some
detail. As far as scaling trust, SEDI doesn't provide any kind of universal trust framework. That's not the
answer. What SEDI provides instead is a foundation that trust frameworks can sit on. So we have some‐
thing that is firm underneath, a firm legal foundation for all of these other things that are going to hap‐
pen. The second help that SEDI can provide is on the economic problem because turns out that the state
already has lots of infrastructure for doing this. They already proof you, part of if you look at your driver's
license, we have a couple of people from Europe here so they may not be familiar with this idea but if you
look at your driver's license which is the only thing we have to prove who we are, there's a little star on
it. You know what that star means?
“REAL ID”. It means the state proofed your identity. So this was started in 2005 when… earlier than
that, maybe 2002… It was right after 911. It happened before 911, because I remember I was CIO when

This was early 2000s, right? So, proofing your identity. And at the time, the motor vehicle division,
the driver's license division said, "We don't want anything to do with identity. We're not an identity
provider. This is not us." Well, let's see how that worked out for them. So, anyway, you have the old star.
They already proofed your identity.
So, let's talk for a minute. I think most people at this point probably have an idea of what SEDI is, but
let's just make sure that we're all on the same page. SEDI doesn't give you an identity. In fact, the SEDI
legislation specifically says: "An individual possesses an identity innate to the individual's existence and
independent of the state. Which identity is fundamental and inalienable." SEDI doesn't give you an iden‐
tity. They're not an identity provider, right? That's a nice term. Instead, what it is, you create your identi‐
fier or identifiers.
The state proofs specific attributes about you, then links those attributes to you in a cryptographic
way, not to you, to this identifier (that you control). In a cryptographic way. So, you can kind of think of
this like a notary, right? The state is endorsing your identity, not giving you an identity. They're just say‐
ing, "Yeah, this identifier, we looked at the person who controls it, and that really is the name and the
birthday and the address and whatever other attributes might be part of your SEDI."
So SEDI not just one credential. Like I said, you create an identifier (or identifiers), and then SEDI is
a credential which links those attributes (that the state has proofed and endorsed) to that identifier. Now
there could be other government credentials that sit on top of that.
Things like driver's licenses. We heard Joe talk about oﬀhighway vehicle operator licenses, fishing li‐
censes. States are veridible credential factories. They just love giving credentials to people for diﬀerent
things, right? So, states have lots of them. Now, some of these will involve guardianship, things like birth
certificates and school records, and the statute includes that idea.
Others will be just things that you get. And, like I said, I've got six up here, but there probably a
thousand of them that states have. Most of us don't care about most of them. But also, other institutions
can give you credentials. So, you could use your SEDI to prove who you are when you go to the bank to
get a credential from your bank. Or you could use your SEDI to prove who you are when you're register‐
ing for school and you would get a school ID. So all of these other credentials are essentially resting on
this foundation that SEDI has provided.

Now, let's talk for a second about the legal underpinnings of SEDI because this is important. I mean first

These are legal rights, not terms of service. This is not a click-through contract that you don't read.
And, if you want to enforce whatever small rights they might have left you in this 60page document, you
have to go to court. These are things that are in statute. They're in law. It also includes selective disclo‐
sure. So the idea that you don't have to disclose everything in a credential or in your SEDI credential in
order to use it. You can just disclose the parts that is necessary. Requires that the system be built on
open standards.
That it's not a proprietary system MDL. So, no vendor lock-in. Anyone can build a conformant wallet
verifier, whatever. Speaking of wallets, it mandates a choice of wallets, right? So, there's not just one app
that the state gives you. And if you don't have that state app, you don't have a SEDI. You can choose dif‐
ferent wallet providers. You can put your SEDI in any of those, move it between them, whatever. It in‐
cludes a duty of loyalty which is like a fiduciary requirement for identity data which is a first. This is rev‐
olutionary in the world of digital identity.
And finally, like I mentioned earlier, it supports the idea, recognizes that many people are wards of a
guardian, and therefore the guardian has to have the rights and the abilities to carry out functions, opera‐

What are they doing? Acting on their behalf. There we go. Acting. That's the word I was looking for.
Nice simple word, not carry out duties. Okay. So, these are all part of the statute and important to why
SEDI creates this kind of legal foundation.

Now, as I mentioned earlier, the problem in this proof gap is that when institutions make errors, you
basically are left to navigate the correction process all on your own. Now, SEDI doesn't mandate that
every institution has to do things a certain way, but it does provide you with a legal foundation. And it
does say that you have enforcable rights related to your SEDI. So you have the right to obtain SEDI cre‐
dentials. You have the right to have its attributes amended as they change, or need correction. You have
protections against arbitrary revocation. There are only certain circumstances where this can be revoked.
There's a complaint, an enforcement mechanism for how you can deal with problems that come up
