What's the technology opportunity here? If you're building decentralized identity infrastructure, SEDI
is an opportunity, but it's not a guarantee. SEDI sets out these requirements: Open standards, decentral‐
ized control, that represent a high bar for how this all has to work. And like I said at the start of the talk,
there's the technical side of it, which is not in any way a small thing, but doing that at scale, meeting the
user experience requirements that we might have, finding the right economic models for the various
parts and pieces that have to work. Those are all extremely diﬃcult problems to solve. So it's not a no-
brainer that “Oh SEDI came along good, it's all solved” and we still have lots of work to do. Timothy.
You've got whole talks about this. – If you in [inaudible] have a legal basis in their giant centralized
digital system, use a lot of cryptography as well component missing and that's the decentralized nature of
SEDI. It's trying to have [inaudible] a legal verifiable basis but also decentralization meaning that you can
have diﬀerent vendors in diﬀerent states and diﬀerent systems and they can issue in one place and verify
in another because they're speaking a common [inaudible] We hope!
– Those systems that's obviously the… But that makes it hard, right? – In contrast with Aadhaar in
India and in contrast with what they're doing in Estonia because they do not have that property and
they're not even trying.
It's a giant centralized system and everybody phones home to the mother ship. – Yeah, absolutely.
–
My point is just having those two components up there describes Aadhaar. That's my point. – Okay.
–
And it does… going back to your point about control because decentralization is about autonomy and

– (Lawrence) Lessig famously said that “code is law” and there's a lot of people who take that to heart
and it is true to the extent that code controls what you can do and what you can't do but I don't think for
identity that idea is quite suﬃcient. That we need more than code, we need this underlying legal founda‐
tion that SEDI provides for individuals.
SEDI answers questions about who has authority to revoke, when can they revoke? That's just one

that we need legal infrastructure which is steady, at least on the road to providing. I don't think this will
be the last SEDI statute. I think there will be more but but nevertheless we're on the road to getting

Phil-Windley-Interview
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · INTERVIEWS · 01:23
236
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So my presentation was about why decentralized identity, first-person identity as I like to call it,
needs a legal foundation. And I spoke to the idea that SEDI, recent legislation that has been passed
in Utah, SEDI stands for State Endorsed Digital Identity, can provide a lot of that legal foundation
that we need to make digital identity work. I don't think it necessarily would surprise many people at this
conference, but one thing I see diﬀerently than many is the need for first-person identity, for identity that
is controlled by the individual rather than provided to them by some other organization. We've been
working on self -sovereign, decentralized identity, first -person identity for since 2015, so 10, 11 years at
this point. What has been missing is the legal foundation that makes it possible. I believe that what I
talked about, the legal foundation for digital identity is not just a nice to have, but a necessary compo‐
nent of a functioning digital ecosystem where people have dignity and the right to act for themselves.

Ryan-Hansen-Interview
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · INTERVIEWS · 01:21
255
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
My name is Ryan Hanson. I'm one of the core developers for the KERI Foundation development
team. Presentation today was about witnesses and watchers and our boot service in the KERI in‐
frastructure that we're deploying and making open to the world. Witnesses and watchers are a
key part of a crucial part of the KERI ecosystem the way it's designed to be used and without them there's
some value in key management at least in terms of being able to have somewhat self-sovereign keys but
without the witness and watcher infrastructure to prove that those things are still valid, that your keys
are still yours, the kind of the whole principle underlying what KERI is all about falls apart. For me per‐
sonally when I first got introduced to KERI, the witness and watcher idea was the thing that really
hooked me in because it doesn't really matter what assertion is made if there's not something that can
prove that that assertion is true, then it ultimately doesn't matter. So in practice the witness and watcher
network infrastructure is critical for being able to assert and prove that something is true and therefore
to make the KERI ecosystem actually work.
Thank you.

SPAC & TSP
Samuel Smith
KERI CONFERENCE APRIL 2026 VIDEOS · TALKS · 40:49
257
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
And so we need to so accountability is about imposing those things. So since any since you can be
correlated anytime you share data with a second party eventually with repeated interactions they're
going to know who you are and then they can share that with anybody they want unless you im‐
pose on them some sort of liability that says I'm afraid or I'm worried about the cost. I'm worried about
something that you've imposed on me some externality that says I'm not going to share this data. I'm go‐
ing to respect your data rights. So any interaction system that does not enable first parties to hold second
parties accountable is fundamentally incompatible with privacy. It's it's not going to be privacy preserv‐
ing. It's going to incentivize them to share your data and violate your privacy rights.
So how we do it? If we understand that the only thing we can do is to create confidential contexts
which are protected from third party surveillance and within those contexts impose liability on the par‐
ties to the confidentiality that changes their behavior. That means that we have to either use a combina‐

veillance by third parties and second parties are held accountable for any information shared with or by
them. It's not just shared with them. It's not just one way disclosure.
We also want to hold them accountable for what they what they do, right? Because safety is about
what is incoming as much as it is what is outgoing. If I go someplace and I want to be in a safe environ‐

that's in here. It's the metadata about here and the Supreme Court has defined it that way. You have pro‐
tections for content data and no protections for non-content metadata. So I can encrypt the content.
I can't encrypt the metadata and therefore the metadata will always be leaky. It'll always provide cor‐
relatable information. So what I have to do is I have to build an artificial construct via tunnels so that I

metadata is content at another layer. And if I do enough layers of tunnels, then I will have a layer where
the metadata is suﬃciently well protected that it can't be surveiled from the outside. So we can make a

And confidentiality is when third parties have no knowledge of what is what was shared. So if the
second party shares with the third party, they broke confidentiality. If the second party shares with the

Court definition of privacy and confidentiality in terms of how we reason about it. And it's important be‐
cause if we lump these all together under one term, then we get confused when we start talking about it.
So I try to be really clear and not get confused about it. So this is the the TSP and I leveled SPAC be‐
cause the name of the protocol when I wrote the white paper was secure privacy authenticity and confi‐
dentiality and the header to the introduction of the protocol says the goal here is to get the best privacy
we can, given we make no compromises on authenticity and confidentiality.
So we start with the strongest possible authenticity, confidentiality and then work backwards to fig‐
ure out what's the best we can do. And the reason for that is, that in my experience, as soon as you com‐
promise on authenticity and confidentiality, you end up with more leakage pass more opportunities and
the net result is you have less privacy overall. Whereas if you start with privacy, you make hard trades
that you can never recover from in on the confidentiality and authenticity part. And that means fraud,
that means identity theft. And if you're worried about somebody knowing who you are because your
metadata got leaked and tracking you, then you have to make a judgment decision saying, "Well, what's
what's more important? they know who you are or they steal all your money," right? For most people,

create other secure contexts and when I create those I can exchange routing data that says move A4 and
B4 to a diﬀerent set of intermediaries.
So now I can use one of these. I can stand this one up and then I can do Do this on steroids because I
say okay the first five packets we're going to use this one we're just going to we're going to create A4 and
B4 and part of the information we exchange is a new set of new pair of intermediaries that we're going to
use and every time we send a message every day we just create a new one. So every day we have so we're
having one-time use contacts and onetime use contacts are highly uncorrelatable because you're minimiz‐
ing the amount of correlatable data because you're creating a new pair of non-correlatable identifiers
within a secure context that is a partition. So you can create partitions on the fly as many as you want
which satisfies all the usable properties that people have for privacy without sacrifice security or confi‐
dentiality or authenticity.
We're done. All right.

The journey begins. So, what I wanted to do on this first ever conference is give a brief history of how we
got here.
Sort of like the foundations. I think most of you have been there through most of the journey. But, I
know there's some people that haven't been, and this talk, will be made available online for other people.
So, I thought this would be a good opportunity to sort of talk about, where it came from.
I started down this path back in 2013, 2014. I wrote a protocol called RAET. It's reliable asyn‐
chronous event transport. It is an end-to-end, peer-to-peer, encrypted, signed, and it was used in produc‐
tion at scale, and it was open source.
So, this was years before most of you have even heard of Signal. Back when Signal wasn't called
Signal, it was called Whisper. But, it was the start of that. So, what was the lesson learned? Well, I could
solve all the problems but key management with RAET.
So, I said, "Okay, that's not a solved problem." So, I already knew that there was a big problem that
had to be solved sometime. <i>OpenReputation</i> I wrote a white paper. It started using self-certify‐
ing identifiers. It had the concept of identity ledger and identity graph.
It defined reputation as a modulator for trust for online interactions. And it brought up the idea that
we wanted to have individual control over the context in which we interact online. Our internet pres‐
ence. And lesson learned from that is that I needed persistent identity. I can't have a good reputation
without a persistent identity, and that's the hard problem of identity because nobody had a solution for it
back then.
<i>Identity System Essentials</i>, this white paper. This is the first draft. Introduced cryptonyms,
built on the SCID model. It provided a formal identity model, primary secondary IDs. Talked about iden‐
tifier management with HD key chains, three degrees of privacy, and it introduced the concept of self-sov‐
ereignty for identity. And the draft paper was shared in the community, and lots of people amplified and
built on the concept of SSI, but as far as I know, that was the first. And I stole it from Phil. Because Phil
Windley had written some blog posts on self-sovereign data, and I said, "Well, we don't just want data to
be self-sovereign. We want our identity to be self-sovereign." That was back in 2016.
The lesson I learned, there's a hard trade space. If you want to trade oﬀ security and privacy and self-
sovereignty and control, you're making some pretty hard trades. Sovrin Foundation. That really changed
the environment for decentralized identity.
The idea was there's going to be one ledger for digital identity. One ledger to rule them all. And it had
persistent identifiers. It had prophylactic [preventive, red.] key rotation, not recoverable key rotation.
That was going to come a while later, but it built on but it established the foundation of managing your
key state so that you'd have persistent identity.
Hard problem, shared governance. That was a hard problem. Spent a lot of time trying to solve that
problem. 2017, the ledger wars. Okay, a lot of you fought in those wars. What happened is SSI was such
a great concept that everybody wanted it. Every ledger wanted to have their own SSI Identity.

KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
So there was every time somebody would have an application that they wanted decentralized identity

That was a really hard lesson to learn. It took several years of fighting the ledger wars to realize that.
The way of KERI. Really the first paper that sort of opened the path was this one in 2018. The three R's

tion. So, it's in that paper.
It says, "How are we going to do key rotation? If we do it the normal way, where we use our signing
key to rotate to a new signing key, if our signing key is compromised, then we lose control of our identity
and that's all of blockchain, by the way.
And that's all of blockchain identity. If you lose control of your signing key, you lose control of your
identity. That's kind of a problem. The other approach is have a rotation key that you use to rotate your
signing key.
But then what happens if your rotation key gets compromised? Well, then you have another rotation
key to rotate the rotation key that rotates the signing key. And I had several cryptographers says that's
best practices. Well, what happens when that one says two is usually enough. You only need two rotation
keys. They'll never get there.
Good idea, but maybe we could do better. Pre-rotation is one-time use only rotation keys. You rotate
your rotation key every time you rotate your signing keys. Pre-rotation and then that also introduced the
idea of having a log of your key state.
Didn't call it a KEL event because we hadn't coined the term KERI, so it couldn't be a KERI event log,

My name is Sam Smith. If anybody can't remember my name, it's because you're more identity chal‐
lenged than my parents thought I would be than they gave me that name. Anyway, that's all right.
Great. I'm going to talk about the trade space of KERI, not in detail, but I'm going to talk about
why we solve the privacy problem using bulk issuance instead of other mechanisms. And I'll give you the
shorthand to the trade space.
KERI's policy is security first always, which means if we're ever in a situation where we're going to
make a trade, and we're going to trade for weaker security or stronger something else, we're not going to
make that trade. We're going to always trade for stronger security. We're not going to say, "Well, that se‐
curity's okay.
We can do this," because the trajectory of technology for security is that every weak trade for security
is a bad trade. May not be today, but in your lifetime it'll be a bad trade. So, let's try to be creative about
solving the other problems without starting oﬀ by saying, "Well, it's easier to solve those problems if we
just throw security under the bus."
Actually say, "You know what? If my constraint is I'm not going to throw security under the bus,
what's the best I can do? And is it good enough?" And that's really the the diﬀerence in the ethos. So for
those of you know, With KERI, each AID controller is its own identity provider.
So, we use this phrase Keys-at-the-Edge. Each edge self-authenticates. What that means is there are
no third-party IDPs, no relying parties, no trusted intermediaries. Every interaction is end-to-end or
edge-to-edge, depending on your your mental model, right? If I have a network and I have people at the
edge trying to get in, it's called an edge.
If I'm trying to communicate across a network, it's called an end, but they're the same thing from
KERI's perspective. In my plenary this morning, I ended with this idea of Reputable Autonomic
Pseudonymity in Control over Confidential Contexts.
The way we solve privacy is with this one, control over confidential contexts. And bulk issuance gives
the user precise control over confidential contexts. So, they get to decide what the reach and scope of
their disclosure are in terms of their correlatability.
We understand that every piece of information that we share over the internet is correlatable. There
is no such thing as no correlation. Unlinkability is not the same as uncorrelatability. So, you know, we
called it Rhapsody [RAPC3].
Let's see how we use ACDCs for this. ACDCs… And I think most of you are familiar, but I recognize
some faces in here that maybe haven't actually sat through, you know, something about ACDC. So, I'm
going to give a couple of just short introductions cuz I've got a lot of detail to go through, and if if you
thought this was going to be a high-level talk like the plenary this morning, I'm really going to disappoint
you.
This is going to go fast. So, think of an ACDC as a graph fragment that has edges that point to other
graph fragments. And so, if you take graph fragments and connect their edges, you get a graph. That's the
point. But, the edges are part of the ACDC.
And the reason for that is the authority and the authentication for each graph fragment includes its
edges. So, you authenticate the attributes which are the node and its edges, and that is an ACDC. And
now I can compose arbitrary data structures from graph fragments because all data can be graphed.
282
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Right?
And the edges (It's called a labeled property graph) because the edges have properties. The edges can
be blinded. They can be private edges. They can have operators. They can be grouped together. They can
be aggregated. They can be used in various ways. The edges have their own set of individual attributes
that are metadata about the way you compose the information in a secure way.
And other approaches to composing information don't allow you to securely compose the information
from multiple sources. They put it all in one data structure that under the hood is actually a graph, right?
If I have a nested set of JSON blocks, it's a graph, but it's not composable in a secure way.
It's all or nothing. And so, ACDC says, "No, that's a bad idea. We need secure composition." ACDCs
are designed to solve problems where you need Perpetually Verifiable Issuances. Can you use an ACDC
with a bare signature with a short crypto .. [inaudible] Absolutely. You don't have to anchor your ACDCs
in a Transaction Event Log or a KEL to make them perpetually verifiable.
But we have to solve the perpetual verifiability problem. So let's solve the hard problem first. So I use
this sometimes that people are familiar with it. If I have a bunch of rocks, a bunch of gravel, and a bunch
of sand, and water, and I want to get all of that into a jar, and get the most that I can get in there, what do
I put in first?
The water, right? I put the water in, right? No, I put the rocks in first, then I put the gravel, then I put
the sand, and then the water fills in the blanks. So ephemeral applications are the easy thing. Everybody
knows how to do ephemeral application. Nobody solved the perpetual verifiable problem before KERI
and ACDC in a decentralized way. Obviously, if you have a database and a central authority to the data‐
base, you can solve any problem you want. It's easy, right? But that's not the hard problem of decentral‐
ized data. The hard problem of decentralized data is how do I solve perpetual issuances without a central‐
ized authority?
Right? Each fragment is separably authenticatable. That means the graph doesn't have a single
source. Every node, every fragment can come from a diﬀerent source. They can all come from one source
or diﬀerent ones. That allows me to do delegation, all sorts of things.
Right? So I have Delegable, Attenuable, Aggrable, Revocable, Transferable Authority. – Can we break
that down? – Okay. Well, I haven't talked about aggregating, but notice that if I have multiple ones going
in one thing, I've aggregated. If I have a chain, that's attenuable delegable. And transferable means that I
can I can change who's in control.
And actually I'll talk about that in my plenary tomorrow. All right. What this allows us to do for veri‐
fiable credentials, which are all about authority, is that we can have high fidelity modeling of real world
authority structures.
Not authority structures that are the best we can do because we have a broken model, which is cur‐

And so, I can have multiple interactions and transactions to negotiate the full set of disclosures and

You've seen demos of contractually protected disclosure. So, that is essential if we're going to have a
comprehensive approach to privacy. Comprehensive approach says you invert the economics. One of the
ways you invert the economics is impose liability on the recipient of the disclosure. They don't get a safe
harbor. They don't get to do things like de-identify the data and then they can do whatever they want
with it. You attach strings to the data and those strings are enforceable which changes their their behav‐
ior. So, we're changing behavior of the recipient of the disclosure, not trying to minimize the disclosure
and then hoping they can't use it against us. We actually say you can't use it against us and if you do, this
is what we're going to do to you.
Right? So, we need to have a contractually protected disclosure and we need to have one that the re‐
cipient will agree to. And that's why we need graduated disclosure so that we can gradually move into a
situation where we're mutually agree. And then we have blindable ACDC state registries. Those are a lit‐

Just nobody calls them that, but if you look at the math, a digital signature is disclosing that some‐
body who knew a private key and a nonce created the signature without disclosing either the private key

proof of entry in the tree without disclosing all of the tree. That's a zero-knowledge proof, by the book.

ACDCs, right? And I gave my talk last night, talked a little bit about what an ACDC is. So I just want to
review it a little bit. An ACDC is this generic graph structure for representing data.
And when we represent data in graphs, we get all kinds of useful properties. And so we've got a ver‐
tex and edges. And we can use those to do all sorts of fun things. But the most important thing that
ACDCs provide is perpetually verifiable issuances. That's the hard problem it solved, that wasn't solved
before. And now that we have perpetually verifiable issuances, we can do all sorts of things we couldn't
have done before.
Each fragment is separately authenticatable. So that provides something that a normal graph data
structure doesn't do. Lots of people have graph data structures, but the whole data structure source from
the same source. Where with ACDCs, every every node in the graph can have a diﬀerent source. So you
can use them for a lot of diﬀerent things that you couldn't use a graph data structure for.
And these are the things that you can do with it. Delegable, attenuable, aggrable, revocable, transfer‐
able, authority. And so we have some talks today. Phil's going to be talking both Phils: Phil [Feairheller]
and Phil Windley about authorizations. We're going to talk about some of the things that now that we
have solved identity, [plus] we solved basic issuances, now we can start to do more complicated things
with ACDCs and so I see the future people are going to find more and more things to use ACDCs for. So
the idea is: with ACDCs we can have high-fidelity modeling of real world authority structures. Can you
guys hear me? I can't tell. Okay.
I can't hear myself because normally I talk really loud and my whole head resonates with my own
sound. So it sounds like nothing's happening. So, one of the things that we can do with ACDCs is we can
bridge. KERI and ACDCs solve the cross trust domain transfer problem in an elegant way and that's a
hard problem to solve.
Recursive Privilege Escalation Attacks (RPEA) are a Negative consequence of using identity systems
that don't solve the cross trust domain transfer problem. Instead, what they do is just increase the size of
the one trust domain because since you can't bridge trust domains, make your trust domain bigger. And
that means now that you can do recursive privilege escalation attacks. If you keep your trust domains
small and bridge them properly, then you don't have recursive privilege escalation attacks. And so with
KERI, we can solve that problem. So the idea is that we have two trust domains and we use ACDCs to
move data trust authority between those domains. (That was a cool eﬀect, right?)
So perpetual identity leads to perpetual issuances means, and I'm going to steal Daniel Hardman's
phrase, lossless trust transfer. We don't lose trust when we use carrying ACDCs to move trust across
trust domains. So if I have ephemeral identity, then I only get ephemeral in issuances. That means every
time I do trust transfer, it's lossy. And that's what we're living with right now in the internet.

Well, I'm defining authorization to access a resource is granted by the resource controller. So I'm go‐
ing to say there's some authority that has authority over a resource and if some other entity wants to get
access to that resource that authority controller grants that access and that access comes through an
authorization.
So each authority is itself an edge in KERI with respect to other authorities resources because it's all
edge-to-edge end-to-end. So each authority can authorize access by any other edge to its resources via an
ACDC. That's the whole point of delegable aggrable authority, is that you grant authority to somebody
and now they can access your resources because you're the authority who can grant that access.
Each issuer in ACDC graph is potentially both a resource authority and an edge. Mutually authenti‐
cated edge accessor and resource authority prevent impersonation attacks. A lot of the impersonation at‐
tacks come because there's a a misconfiguration of how those two talk with respect to each other. You
look at how VPNs .., A common VPN problem is that I have a VPN and I give an edge access through the
VPN, but the VPN isn't mutually authenticating in a strong way.
So, the edge thinks that it's talking to the VPN, but it's not. It's talking to an impersonation of the
VPN. So, when it grants access, it thinks, "Oh, okay, I can now share my other credentials to what's ever
on the other side of the VPN." But I'm not on the VPN I thought I was. Because I don't have strong mutu‐

back to the issuing authority we can do a reversion of authority. So let me give the example. Let's say that
I'm a lieutenant of a company and I take one of the members of the company and I give him an order and
I say you're going to go over there, right? And he goes over there, right? He has authority to do it. I told
him to do something. Let's say that I I resend that order. He can't do that anymore.
What do I do at that point? The authority to do that still rests in me, the lieutenant. I can go over
there and do it or I can authorize whatever it is myself. So, the authority always reverts to me as the
higher authority. So what we do is that when a subordinate or a delegate loses authority because we've
revoked it, any outstanding issuances that were validly issued that we want to dynamically revoke the re‐
vocation now becomes the responsibility of the delegator. It just reverts back to the delegator. And be‐
cause we have an edge, we know who the delegator is. And so all we have to do is make a policy change
that says in this case where I revoked .., his authority right here.
So you see this one over here is issued, I can't revoke because he's been revoked over here in number
five. But guess who can revoke it? See the line that comes back here? He can revoke it. And a verifier just
needs to know that's the policy.
It just looks it up and says, "Oh, okay. The approximate delegate doesn't have authority because it's
his authority has been revoked. The chain's broken." But any subsequent actions on that ACDC are still
the responsibility of the delegator. And so the delegator can revoke it. So now we can have dynamically
revocable credentials and break the chain and still dynamically revoke them. Yeah.
Well, well, since there's always edges, you can the reversion of authority can go all the way up to the
top of the chain. You can break the leak as many times you want and you just keep you just rinse and re‐
peat all you just ascend the chain. So now you have an elegant solution for dynamically revocable delegat‐
ed credentials all the way down. You can break the chain any number of times and still control those cre‐
dentials dynamically.

Here's why. Second parties to repeated interactions can correlate the first parties. If I interact with
you, even though we're both anonymous, the interaction themselves is enough to correlate. So guess
what? I can't interact and have privacy.
I can't. So any interaction system that does not enable first parties to hold second parties accountable
is fundamentally incompatible with privacy. You can't get there unless you start with accountability. And
you don't get privacy by hiding, you get privacy by interacting with digital refuges Surveillance asylums

That's the whole point. If I want to interact with him or her, right, and I can hold him accountable,

Suite, allows us to create and control safe relationships where interactions protected from surveillance by
third parties and second parties are held accountable for any information shared with or by them.
My breakout talk today will be talking about the TSP protocol and how we can do that. Keep going.
We only got a minute. Okay. You can have some of my time. All right. Okay. So when I do this analysis, I
always use this model.
They say "All models are wrong, but some models are useful." I find this a very useful model. Any in‐
teraction on the internet involves the first party and second party. I can encrypt the communications be‐
tween the first party and the second party so that the content isn't observable by any other party.
But if I'm using the internet, what I can't protect is the metadata of the fact that I sent data over the
internet. It has to be routable. Routing tables are public. Routing information is subpoenable. All of that
metadata is not protected.
The Supreme Court even said so. Right? Reasonable expectation of privacy is limited to content, not
non-content metadata. Non-content metadata is how you get from first party to second party. If I can cor‐
relate out from the metadata who the first party and second party are, I've already defeated privacy in
general. There's no privacy on the internet ever. Never has been, never will be. If you use that definition,
right? But what I can do is I have privacy where I can make it so that I that they don't know who the first
party and the second party are because the first party and second party they see aren't the first party and
second party that matter.
303
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
And so the basic idea is: I don't use clandestine means to get privacy. I use covert means and covert
means you have a cover identity that basically provides a layer of protection to some activity that you're
doing that isn't observable. Clandestine just means you're not observable. But any information that's
broadcast, is eventually observable. So clandestine can only be used for ephemeral applications. And I
spent 20 years and 20, you know, millions of dollars of federal money building clandestine surveillance
mechanisms and at some point we said, "Oh, we got to make them covert because we need Persistent
Lliteral Surveillance;" that was the name of the program. And when we went from ephemeral to persis‐
tent surveillance, we had to change our concept of operations.
And that's what I'm saying is we have to change that. So this is the model. And then and to get from
first party, second party, I have to have intermediaries. The intermediaries can surveil stuﬀ that a third
party can't. Your ISP is an intermediary.
So given that model, these are the two principles that we use. One is end-to-end verifiability which
tells me that I know who the parties are. The parties know each other. If I have KERI then I get ambient
verifiability any day anywhere anytime by anybody of who it came from. So I can hold the source account‐
able right; non-repudiation.
Now the dual of that, is what I call 'Only At End Viewability'. I need to be able to send information to
somebody and have reasonable confidence that they're the only ones that got it. Because the only way I
can hold the second party accountable is if I know they're the leaker of data.
Because if I send encrypted data to a second party that they can decrypt, they have the data. I no
longer have any ability to restrict where that data goes. There's no technological means that allows me to
protect that data once the second party has it. I have to trust the second party. So I have no privacy of any
information I share with the second party if the second party can share it with somebody else. So there's
no longer technology here. It's all legal, regulatory, and economic.
So if any information that I interact with or share in the internet once it gets to the second party the
second party wants to collude with anybody they want to then I have no privacy. The only privacy only
comes if I can trust the second party to respect the terms and conditions, which is called confidentiality,
of the information I give them. So what I need is a mechanism for sending information from here to
there that is asymmetric. So I need to use hybrid public key encryption so that I have asymmetric encryp‐
tion and I send it to one end. I don't use group encryption. I don't do shared secret encryption. I use hy‐
brid public key which is technically shared secret encryption but the key on my end is ephemeral. So I
have a reasonable confidence that only the targeted one got it and I can in include information in it that
makes it unique to that end which they can get rid of if they if they're smart but it makes it really hard for
them. So I said reasonable confidence. What that means is that I have this property of End Only
Viewability. The end that I intend to is the only one that can view it. So there's "one data, one where, one
time, by one body," and that means that it's undeniable. Now they can deny it.
Plausible deniability. I can't prove, may not be able to prove it, but I know. And guess what I do when
I know? I break the interaction. I exclude them from my interaction profile. I stop talking to them. I stop
sharing data with them because I can't trust them. So the whole point is: I need to be able to control who
I talk to and who talks to me. And I need to do it in a mechanism that allows me to decide that and to
control the boundaries of that. And that's the way I do it. And so the TSP protocol lets you decide with

nectivity. It is a bother, and once you're done with it, you go to the next, and you repeat that entire

contention that we have for the entire industry is that that's a really, really bad idea because we don't
want to centralize power into a few diﬀerent, you know, private organizations as they move forward.
Now, I don't think the clearing houses are going anywhere.

KERI Infrastructure & the 5

All right. Good afternoon almost. My name is Thomas Mayfield. I'm here from the Cardano
Foundation. And I'm going to be speaking today about Veridian. And usually when I'm in front of
this majority of this group of people, I am demonstrating what we've been building.
But today I have the honor of actually providing a bit more of a educational or academic. Is it not loud
enough Jared? Testing better. Excellent. Okay. So my name is Thomas Mayfield. I'm from the Cardano
Foundation. And I'm here going to be speaking a bit about Veridian. And usually when I'm in front of this
group of people, I'm demonstrating the tech that we've been building at the Cardano Foundation.
But today I have the honor of actually providing a bit more of an educational overview of KERI and
more specifically speaking about the concept of the five Ws. I will intertwine elements of Veridian
throughout the talk. But I think the majority of this audience, it's probably going to be Already content
you're very familiar with, but hopefully for the viewers that are going to be watching this online and at
home, it's a more of an informative content for them.
I'm going to put my timer on so that way Henk doesn't have to give me a reminder. All right. So, to‐
day I'm going to walk you through the KERI infrastructure using a framework known as the five W's.
Wallet, witness, watcher, web, and wizard. And by the end of this talk, you'll understand not just what
each of these roles are, but how they work together as a system and also how Veridian is building all of
this in the open. So, let's start with the problem. Digital identity today is borrowed. When you create an
account, get a credential or receive a certificate. You don't actually own it. The issuer does, the registry
does, or the platform does. And if that platform shuts down, that registry gets compromised or that is‐
suer revokes access, your identity eﬀectively disappears with it.
You are eﬀectively a tenant, not an owner of your digital identity. But KERI changes this. With KERI,
your identity is rooted in cryptographic keys that you control. No registry needed, no platform dependen‐
cy. It's portable, verifiable, and it is yours.
So what is KERI exactly? And I don't think anyone's actually said the acronym in the last few days. So
KERI stands for key event receipt infrastructure and there are three core concepts that make this work.
The first is self-certifying identifiers and what this means is that your identifier is derived directly from
your cryptographic keys. There is no registry or registrar that grants you your identity.
The second concept is that every critical action you take with that identifier, such as creating it or ro‐
tation those controlling cryptographic keys, all of these critical events are signed and chained into a tam‐
per-evident log. And the third concept is everyone can verify your identity by replaying the same tamper-
evident log. There is no need for phone home to a central repository or authority. And if you'd like to un‐
derstand a bit more about no phone home, I encourage you to visit to visit nophonehome.com and you
can read a bit more about this.
So this combination of concepts, this is what gives us something that no other identity system
achieves and that is truly portable trust. So let's make this concrete. In KERI, your identifier is called an
autonomic identifier or an AID. And this is a cryptographic identifier that you generate. Its authority
comes from the keys that you made. And no one else or entity issues that to you. Every significant action
that the AID conducts gets recorded in a Key Event Log and that is called a KEL.
Your inception event, this is when you create your AID. The rotation events that change the AID's
controlling cryptographic keys or interaction events that anchor data that is critical to your AID. Each
and every event is cryptographically signed.
320
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Each and every event is cryptographically signed with the previous one. And this is what creates a
tamper-evident chain. If anyone alters a single event in this chain, the signatures break and the tamper‐
ing is immediately detectable.
If you're familiar with blockchain technology, this is the same hashchain data structure that makes
blockchain secure or immutable. Now that you understand the foundations, AIDs and KELs, let's talk
about the infrastructure that makes it all work at scale. And we've organized these into five roles. And
conveniently, Sam has started them all with a letter W. So, let's move on to the wallet. And this is where
your identity lives. It's the holder's home. The wallet generates and rotates the cryptographic keys. It
stores the verifiable credentials. In KERI, these are known as ACDCs. We'll talk a bit about this later. The
wallet signs, presentations, and interaction events. And it collects all of this and it combines all of this
with device grade security. This includes biometrics, secure enclaves, and anything else that occurs on
the hardware device itself. The key insights here are: the wallet is the only component that the holder di‐
rectly controls. The vital functions of the wallet in KERI are key management and signing.
At Veridian, we've built an open-source mobile-first KERI wallet that is production ready and avail‐
able today. You can download that from the Apple iOS stores as well as Android Play Store. and it is also
available on the privacy-preserving operating system Graphine OS.
Witnesses are the second W in the 5W framework. They are designated services that receive, sign,
and republish your key events. You can think of these as notaries for your KEL. They give your event log
a highly available home so verifiers can access it when your wallet is oﬄine. They produce receipts that
prove the specific event has been seen at a specific time.
And through redundancy of these witnesses, we can resist censorship. Critically, you choose your wit‐
nesses. They are not selected or imposed by the system on you. And at Veridian, we operate witness
pools that you can use directly or you can host yourself in your own infrastructure.
The third W is the watcher. Watchers are independent observers. They are critical to KERI. Their job
is to detect duplicity. Meaning: they catch anyone who is trying to present two conflicting versions of the
same Key Event Log.
This is KERI's answer to the double spend problem that you have in blockchain. But they do it for
identity. Watchers give verifiers confidence that the state they are seeing is the latest legitimate state of
an identifier.
They run separately from issuers and holders which means they are truly independent. This is what
turns 'trust me!' into 'check for yourself' or 'verify for yourself'. And at Veridian, we are deploying highly
available watcher infrastructure services in the second half of this year. And I would be remiss if I did not
pay attention and show gratitude to the HealthKERI for releasing the watcher and wallets to open
source.
The fourth W is web. This is the discovery and transport layer of KERI. This is how parties find one
another and how they exchange events and credentials and they do so through something known as an
OOBI. This is an out-of-band introduction. You can think of these as sharable links that bootstrap trust
between parties.
321
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
Eﬀectively, these can also be assumed to be QR codes that establish an initial connection and from
there mailboxes and endpoints handle the messaging moving forward. The design of KERI is transport
agnostic. So today it runs over HTTP, but the protocol doesn't really care about the transport. It can run
over Bluetooth, over NFC. In fact, it can run across anything.
And it's interoperable across diﬀerent wallets and agents. Veridian provides hosted endpoints and
sandboxes from our data center in Switzerland, including infrastructure deployment guides for those that
are interested in deploying.
The fifth and final W, the wizard. This is how we achieve one-click KERI. For example, the majority of
users are not going to understand how to set their witness threshold. Wizards abstract low-level details
of KERI and they provide suitable defaults that ensure we make KERI accessible but safe for users.
For developers, we expose highlevel easy to understand APIs to build on. And in short, the wizard
bridges the protocol to the product. Without KERI, sorry, without wizards, ... Yes, without Wizards,
KERI is a powerful protocol.
With Wizards, KERI is a product that you can ship to market. And at Veridian, we oﬀer one-click on‐
boarding, witness selection, and automated schema discovery. Now, this is the whole picture. This is how
they all fit together.
Wallets hold the keys and credentials. Witnesses attest to events. Watchers verify integrity. Wizards
orchestrate operations and the web connects them all and provides scalability. Each of these five roles are
independently operated, independently verified and uniquely can be independently replaced.
So that's the friendly overview. We're going to delve a little bit deeper and I want to cover four con‐
cepts that take KERI from an interesting protocol to a production grade infrastructure. And those four
concepts are pre-rotation, delegation, multi-signature control, and ACDCs.
So we'll start with pre-rotation. And this is one of KERI's most elegant concepts. In fact, it was proba‐
bly the main concept that enabled me to influence the board of the Cardano Foundation to allow me to
build on KERI. Pre-rotation, as I mentioned, is one of the most elegant concepts of KERI. At inception of
an AID, the moment you create it, you don't just commit to your current keys. You also include a hash of
your next-keys set. Those next keys stay oﬄine.
They remain unused only until you need to rotate your controlling key. If an attacker steals your cur‐
rent signing keys, they cannot forge a valid rotation event. And because they cannot do that and because
they don't have your next keys, but because you have committed a hash of your next keys, that rotation is
already locked in and you can verify someone is legitimate controller of an identifier without publishing
the public key. This is extremely important.
This gives KERI something called post- compromise recovery. Even after a key compromise, you can
regain control. And because of this commitment, using this hash, we have resistance to future quantum-
computing attacks. There is no other digital identity system that oﬀers this level of key management
today.
Multi- signature control. This ensures no single point of compromise for high value identifiers. An
AID can require M of N signers. For example, two or three board members must sign to rotate the orga‐
nization's root key. The threshold can be weighted for governance scenarios. Rotation events are also re‐
322
KERI Conference April 2026 Videos
spective of the threshold rules. This means you cannot bypass multi- signature control even during a ro‐
tation event.
This is critical for enterprise AIDs where a single compromise no longer means the entire organiza‐
tion is now compromised; and 'game over'. And Veridian supports multi-signature control coordination,
out-of-the box, with our Veridian wallet and our cloud agents. And we handle the ceremony of gathering
the signatures across multiple parties seamlessly for you.
ACDCs: authentic chained data containers. This is KERI's answer to verifiable credentials, but they go
much further than the typical verifiable credential or VC that exists in the decentralized identity space.
Each ACDC is signed and anchored into an issuer's KEL. So, its provenance is always verifiable.
Schemas are composable and versionable, so you can build complex credential types from simpler
ones. An ACDC supports graduated disclosure where you can share only the minimum information
needed for a given interaction. And you'll notice on the slide that the credentials chain back through the
schemas and issuers to a Root of Trust.
This is what authentic chained really means. Every link in the chain is verifiable. And Veridian wallet
allows you to store and present ACDCs natively. It's also important to mention that although the wallet
is storing a copy of your ACDC, the way that ACDCs work, the way that they are presented, if you lose
your wallet, you are still able to recover those ACDCs because it is eﬀectively the keys that allow you to
prove ownership of those credentials. So, as long as you have a backup of that data somewhere, you can
regain those credentials or you can request that they are reissued to you. This is extremely powerful.
Delegation of authority. This would probably be the second reason I was able to influence the
Cardano Foundation to pursue this technology. Delegation is how KERI handles organizational identity.
An AID can delegate authority through ACDCs down from a Root of Trust. GLEIF's vLEI system uses ex‐
actly this pattern for organizational identity within the financial sector. A qualified vLEI issuer, a QVI, re‐
ceives a delegation from the GLEIF's root AID, and then has the authority to issue legal entity credentials
to other organizations.
That same authority can also be revoked at any time. Delegated AIDs also enable top-down hierarchi‐
cal recovery. For example, this is how GLEIF can recover a compromised QVI's AID. With KERI delega‐
tion, the trust chain is always auditable. You can always trace any delegated AID back to its root.
So, at Veridian, we are building all five of the W's. The open- source mobile wallet. We are hosting
witness pools and self-hosting options for those who are interested. We are committed to releasing pub‐
lic and private watcher services this year. We have hosted endpoints, UB resolution, credential manage‐
ment services, secure KERI tunnels and identity access management integration modules.
We have enterprise onboarding, provisioning services, deployment and infrastructure automation as
well as disaster recovery. All of this is open source. All of this is production grade and it is built in the
open by the Cardano Foundation. I'd now like to share with you an overview of what it is Veridian is aim‐
ing to achieve for the second half of this year and into the years to come.
Can you trust who's on the other side? Institutions cannot verify who they're children with strangers.
AI agents execute without a story Today we cannot answer the most basic questions who's on the other
side and who authorizes to act. Veridian has built the answer to both an indentifier you can verify a trust‐
